They intersect the argument with `[0, +∞]`, return the empty interval if that holds no real above 0, and otherwise `[f_rd(a), f_ru(b)]`, where MPFR gives -∞ for a zero and +∞ for +∞. `closed_inc_correct` and `dec_closed_inc_correct` in `ClosedDom.v` do the same for `asin` and `acosh`, which intersect the argument with `[-1, 1]` or `[1, +∞]` and return `[f_rd(a), f_ru(b)]` for a nonempty intersection `[a, b]`, and `acos_correct` and `dec_acos_correct` for `acos`, which is decreasing and returns `[acos_rd(b), acos_ru(a)]`. `Interval::atanh` intersects the argument with `[-1, 1]`, as MPFR gives -∞ and +∞ for -1 and 1, and returns the empty interval if the intersection holds no real of `(-1, 1)`. `Interval::cosh` returns `[cosh_rd(b), cosh_ru(a)]` if `b < 0`, `[cosh_rd(a), cosh_ru(b)]` if `a > 0`, and `[1, cosh_ru(max(-a, b))]` otherwise, as `cosh` is even and increasing on `[0, +∞)`, with its least value 1 at 0.

The decorated versions are the two arms of `impl_dec!`. The first passes the decoration of `x` to `set_dec`, and the second, for `acos`, `acosh`, `asin`, `atanh`, `ln`, `log10` and `log2`, the minimum of it and the decoration that `Interval::<f>_impl` returns, which is `com` if `x` is a nonempty subset of the domain, and `trv` otherwise. `elem_dec_correct` and `elem_dec_impl_correct` in [`ElemDec.v`](theories/ElemDec.v) show for both, once, that this is the decoration that IEEE 1788 asks for. Both apply `elem_set_dec_trv_com`, which takes the decoration `trv` or `com`, `com` for the first arm, and which follows from `elem_set_dec_correct` there, the generic theorem about the decorations of a function of one real, which does for one real what `elem2_set_dec_correct` does for two (see `pow` below), with the decoration that `dec_holds_dom` describes in place of `dec_holds`. The strongest decoration is `trv` if `x` is empty or has a real outside the domain, `com` if `x` is a nonempty, bounded subset of the domain and the result is common, and `dac` otherwise, as each point function is continuous on its domain, relative to it, so that `asin` is continuous at -1 and 1, as IEEE 1788 counts it. `asin`, `acos` and `acosh` test that `x` is a subset of `[-1, 1]` or `[1, +∞]` with `subset`, and `atanh` and the logarithms that it is in `(-1, 1)` or `(0, +∞)` with `interior` of `[-1, 1]` or `[0, +∞]`, which hold exactly when the reals of `x` are reals of the other interval, or each has a neighbourhood in it (`subset_mem` and `interior_mem` in [`Interior.v`](theories/Interior.v)). `set_dec` lowers `com` to `dac` for a result that is not common, so the only case left is a `com` `x` that is unbounded and gives a common result. `com_common` rules it out for `atan`, `exp`, `exp10`, `exp2` and `tanh`, whose results can be common for an unbounded `x`, such as `exp([-∞, 0]) = [0, 1]`. For the others it cannot happen. `sinh` and `asinh` have no bound at either end, and `cosh` gives +∞ for either infinity, so an unbounded `x` gives an unbounded result. The second arm passes `com` on only for an `x` in the domain, which is bounded for `asin`, `acos` and `atanh`, and which, if it is unbounded, has the upper bound +∞ for the logarithms and `acosh`, which they take to +∞.

`cos_correct` in [`Cos.v`](theories/Cos.v) shows that `Interval::cos`, which computes its bounds with MPFR, returns the tightest interval that contains the image of its argument under Stdlib's `cos`, and `dec_cos_correct` that `DecInterval::cos` decorates it as IEEE 1788 asks, with the hypothesis `com_common`. `cos_encloses` states only that the result contains the image, which is all that `cos_valid` in `ValidElem.v` needs. All three assume `sin_ok` as well as `cos_ok`, as `Interval::cos` calls `sin_rd` and `cos_ru` where it reduces the argument exactly (below):

```coq
Theorem cos_encloses :
  forall emin emax m has_fma first x,
  range_ok emin emax -> get_d_ok emin emax m -> cos_ok emin emax m -> sin_ok emin emax m ->
  valid x ->
  exists y, Interval.cos m has_fma first x = Some y /\
    encloses (image_dom real_dom cos x) y.

Theorem cos_correct :
  forall emin emax m has_fma first x,
  range_ok emin emax -> get_d_ok emin emax m -> cos_ok emin emax m -> sin_ok emin emax m ->
  valid x ->
  exists y, Interval.cos m has_fma first x = Some y /\
    is_tightest (image_dom real_dom cos x) y.

Theorem dec_cos_correct :
  forall emin emax m has_fma first x,
  range_ok emin emax -> get_d_ok emin emax m -> cos_ok emin emax m -> sin_ok emin emax m ->
  valid (itv x) -> com_common x ->
  exists y, DecInterval.cos m has_fma first x = Some y /\
    if DecInterval.is_nai x then y = DecInterval.NAI
    else
      is_tightest (image_dom real_dom cos (itv x)) (itv y) /\
      exists d, strongest_dom real_dom d cos (itv x) (itv y) /\
        dec y = dec_min d (dec x).
```

`encloses s y` in `Ieee1788.v` says that `y` is `valid` and contains `s`, the accuracy that IEEE 1788 calls valid. `sin_encloses`, `sin_correct` and `dec_sin_correct` in [`Sin.v`](theories/Sin.v) state the same for `sin`, with the hypotheses `sin_ok` and then `cos_ok`. `tan_encloses`, `tan_correct` and `dec_tan_correct` in [`Tan.v`](theories/Tan.v) state it for `tan` on its domain `tan_dom`, with `tan_ok`, `sin_ok` and `cos_ok`. `Interval::tan` calls neither `f64::max` nor `f64::min`, so it takes no `first`, and `dec_tan_correct` needs no `com_common`:

```coq
Theorem dec_tan_correct :
  forall emin emax m has_fma x,
  range_ok emin emax -> get_d_ok emin emax m -> tan_ok emin emax m ->
  sin_ok emin emax m -> cos_ok emin emax m -> valid (itv x) ->
  exists y, DecInterval.tan m has_fma x = Some y /\
    if DecInterval.is_nai x then y = DecInterval.NAI
    else
      is_tightest (image_dom tan_dom tan (itv x)) (itv y) /\
      exists d, strongest_dom tan_dom d tan (itv x) (itv y) /\
        dec y = dec_min d (dec x).
```

`Interval::cos` of a nonempty `x = [a, b]` computes `q = floor(x / PI)` with the `/` and `floor` of intervals proved above, and reads its bounds `qa` and `qb`; `sin` and `tan` divide by `FRAC_PI_2` instead. `PI_bounds` in [`PiBounds.v`](theories/PiBounds.v) shows that the bounds of `Interval::PI` are below and above π. `PI_Q` there bounds π by two rationals `10^-18` apart, from Hutton's formula `π/4 = 2 atan(1/3) + atan(1/7)` and the bounds of the partial sums of its series in Stdlib (`PI_2_3_7_ineq` in `Machin.v`), which `vm_compute` compares with these rationals in `Q`, and the rationals lie strictly between the bounds of `Interval::PI`, which `vm_compute` checks too. The bounds of `Interval::FRAC_PI_2` are those of `Interval::PI` halved. For an interval `C` of finite floats `[lo, hi]` with `1 <= lo < c < hi` (`trig_const`), `trig_reduce` in [`TrigReduce.v`](theories/TrigReduce.v) shows that an infinite bound of `x` gives an infinite bound of `q`, a finite `a` gives an integer `qa` with `qa * c <= a`, strictly unless `qa` is 0, and a finite `b` an integer `qb` with `b < (qb + 1) * c`, so that every real of `x` is in `[qa * c, (qb + 1) * c)`. It uses `trig_quot_lo` and `trig_quot_hi` in [`TrigQuot.v`](theories/TrigQuot.v), which bound the tightest quotient `x / C` below by `a / t` for `t` = `c`, `lo` and `hi`, and above by `b / c`. `n = qb - qa` is rounded to nearest, but the tests `n == 0.0`, `n <= 1.0`, `n < 2.0` and so on decide the exact difference, as rounding to nearest is monotone and the integers that `n` is compared with are floats (`n_small`), and `n` is +∞ if a bound of `x` is infinite (`n_inf`). If `a == b`, `n` is 0 instead. `rem_euclid_2(qa)` and `qa.rem_euclid(4.0)` give the remainders of `qa` by 2 and 4 (`rem_euclid_2_int` and `rem_euclid_4_int`).

Each branch returns one of six forms: `[f_rd(a), f_ru(b)]`, where `f` increases, `[f_rd(b), f_ru(a)]`, where it decreases, `[min(f_rd(a), f_rd(b)), 1]` around a maximum, `[-1, max(f_ru(a), f_ru(b))]` around a minimum, `[-1, 1]` and `[-∞, +∞]`. [`TrigBranch.v`](theories/TrigBranch.v) proves once, for any `f`, when each form contains the image and when it is the tightest interval that does. `cos` is monotone on each `[k * π, (k + 1) * π]`, `sin` on each `[k * π/2, (k + 1) * π/2]`, and `tan` increases between two of its poles, the odd multiples of π/2 ([`TrigPieces.v`](theories/TrigPieces.v)). Every branch contains the image of an `x` with `a != b`, as the pieces that meet `[qa * c, (qb + 1) * c)`, or, where the three functions take `q` and `n` from the quadrants (below), `[ka * c, (kb + 1) * c)` for the floors `ka` and `kb` of `a / c` and `b / c`, are the ones that the branch's form allows for. When `tan` returns `com` for an `x` with `a != b`, `x` holds no pole: if `qa` is even, `n` is 0, so `x` lies in `[qa * π/2, (qa + 1) * π/2)`, between two poles, and if `qa` is odd, `n` is at most 1, `qa * π/2 < a` keeps out the pole `qa * π/2`, and `b < (qb + 1) * π/2 <= (qa + 2) * π/2` the next one. Where `tan` takes `q` and `n` from the quadrants (below), the same holds with the floors of `a / (π/2)` and `b / (π/2)` in place of `qa` and `qb`. A singleton `[a, a]` gives `n = 0`, whatever `qa` is, so a monotone form, which is the tightest for one real, for `tan` because `a` is not a pole: `tan_dom_f64` derives this from `pi_irrational` below, as every finite float is rational. An argument at least a period wide gives `[-1, 1]` or `[-∞, +∞]`, which is the tightest, as the argument holds reals where `cos`, or `sin`, is 1 and -1 (`cos_wide`, `sin_wide`), or reals where `tan` is above and below any bound (`tan_wide`). If `qa` and `qb` are the floors `ka` and `kb` of `a / c` and `b / c` (`trig_reduce_exact`), each branch returns the tightest form for `x`.

`x / C` only encloses the quotients `r / c`, as `Interval::PI` and `Interval::FRAC_PI_2` are one ulp wide and the division rounds outward, so `qa` can be below `ka` when `a` is just above a multiple of `c`, and `qb` above `kb` when `b` is just below one. So the three functions keep the `q` and `n` of `qa` and `qb` only in three cases, and otherwise compute them from the quadrants of `a` and `b`, which give the exact floors modulo 4. The first is `n = 0`: either `a == b`, where a monotone form is the tightest whatever `qa` is (above), or `qa = qb`, so that `qa = ka = kb = qb`, as `qa <= ka <= kb <= qb`. The second is `b - a`, rounded to nearest, at least 8, which it is for an unbounded `x` too, as it is then +∞: `x` then holds two reals at least `2 * π` apart (`trig_wide` in [`TrigCert.v`](theories/TrigCert.v)). For `tan`, it is `b - a` at least 4, where `x` is unbounded or more than `π` wide (`tn_sub_wide` in `Tan.v`). The third is that `floors_exact` says that the floors are exact: it divides the singletons `[a, a]` and `[b, b]` by `C`, and tests that the floor of the upper bound of the first quotient is `qa` and that of the lower bound of the second is `qb`. `floors_exact_ok` shows that `a < (qa + 1) * c` and `qb * c <= b` then, as the first quotient contains `a / c` and the second `b / c`, which with `trig_reduce` makes `qa` and `qb` the floors of `a / c` and `b / c`. Otherwise, `quadrant` returns `k mod 4` for the floor `k` of `r / (π/2)`, for a finite float `r`, from the signs of `sin_rd(r)` and `cos_ru(r)` (`quadrant_correct` in [`TrigQuad.v`](theories/TrigQuad.v)). Rounding down keeps `sin r < 0` and never makes a `sin r >= 0` negative, and rounding up keeps `cos r > 0` and never makes a `cos r <= 0` positive, so the tests decide the signs of `sin r` and `cos r` exactly, however close `r` is to a multiple of π/2. On `[k * π/2, (k + 1) * π/2)`, these signs give `k mod 4`, except at the lower end for `k mod 4` = 2 or 3, where `sin r` or `cos r` is 0: an odd multiple of π, or of π/2, which no float is, as π is irrational (`qd_not_kpi` in `TrigQuad.v`, and `tan_dom_f64`). `quadrants(a, b)` returns the quadrant of `a` and a count `n` that is `kb - ka` if that is less than 4, and at least 4 otherwise, for the floors `ka` and `kb` of `a / (π/2)` and `b / (π/2)` (`quadrants_correct` in [`TrigCount.v`](theories/TrigCount.v)). The quadrants give `d = (kb - ka) mod 4`, so `kb - ka` is `d`, where `b - a < (d + 1) * π/2`, or at least `d + 4`, where `b - a > (d + 3) * π/2`. The float `2 * d + 3` lies between the two, more than 0.4 away from each, so `b - a`, rounded to nearest, is at most `2 * d + 3` in the first case and above it in the second. `int_tests` shows that the tests of the branches on such an `n` are those on the integer that it holds. `sin` takes the quadrant of `a` as `q`, `tan` its remainder by 2, and `cos`, for the quadrant `j` of `a`, `q = floor(j / 2)` and `n = floor((j % 2 + n) / 2)`, as `floor(r / π) = floor(floor(r / (π/2)) / 2)`. `quadrant` calls `sin_rd` and `cos_ru`, so the theorems about `cos`, `sin` and `tan` assume both `sin_ok` and `cos_ok`. They need no hypothesis about MPFR that `Mpfr.v` did not already state, and no bound on how close a float can be to a multiple of π/2.

`DecInterval::cos` and `DecInterval::sin` pass the decoration of `x` to `set_dec`, as the first arm of `impl_dec!` does. `cos` and `sin` are defined and continuous everywhere, and their results are bounded, so `elem_set_dec_trv_com` in `ElemDec.v` with `com`, applied to the tightest interval that `cos_correct` and `sin_correct` give, gives the strongest decoration for every `x` that `com_common` allows. `DecInterval::tan` passes the minimum of it and the decoration that `Interval::tan_impl` returns, `com` for an `x` that `tan_impl` finds to lie between two poles and `trv` otherwise, which holds, as `tan` is continuous on its domain, relative to it, and `dec_tan_correct` applies `elem_set_dec_trv_com` with that decoration. It is the strongest, as `[-∞, +∞]` is returned, decorated `trv`, only for an `x` that holds a pole. `tan_impl` returns `com` only for a bounded `x`, so `com_common` is not needed. `Interval::cos` and `Interval::sin` call `f64::max` or `f64::min` at most once on each path, so the theorems hold for every value of `first`, as for `cosh`.

`pi_irrational` in [`PiIrrational.v`](theories/PiIrrational.v) shows that π is irrational, which Stdlib does not prove:

```coq
Theorem pi_irrational : forall p q : Z, (0 < q)%Z -> PI <> IZR p / IZR q.
```

That π is not a dyadic rational `m × 2^e` would not be enough for `tan_dom_f64` and `qd_not_kpi`, which together show that no finite float is `k * π/2` for an integer `k` other than 0: such a float `x` would make π the rational `2 * x / k`, whose denominator need not be a power of 2. Ruling out such an `x` numerically would need a verified search over every float from π/2 to 2^1024, as in worst-case argument reduction, with π to more than 1000 bits: every float `x` at least `2^1023` is within π/4 of some `k * π/2` with `k` above `2^1022`, which puts `2 * x / k` within `2^-1021` of π.

The proof is Niven's (I. Niven, "A simple proof that π is irrational", Bull. Amer. Math. Soc. 53 (1947), p. 509), with the mean value theorem in place of his integral, so that it needs no integrals. If `PI = a / b` with `b > 0`, the polynomial `f(x) = x^n (a - b x)^n` is symmetric about π/2, as `x (a - b x) = b x (π - x)`, so its `k`-th derivative at π is `(-1)^k` times that at 0, which is `n!` times an integer (`nv_d_sym` and `nv_d_int` in [`NivenSym.v`](theories/NivenSym.v)). With `F = f - f'' + f'''' - ...`, the alternating sum of its even derivatives up to the order `2 n`, and `G(x) = F'(x) sin x - F(x) cos x`, `G' = f sin` (`nv_G_deriv`), as `F'' + F = f`, the derivative of order `2 n + 2` being 0 (`nv_d_high`). `G(π) - G(0) = F(π) + F(0) = 2 F(0)` (`nv_G_ends`), which is `2 n!` times an integer. By the mean value theorem (Stdlib's `MVT_cor2`), it is also `π f(c) sin c` for some `c` in `(0, π)`, which is above 0 and at most `π (π a)^n`, as `0 < c (a - b c) < π a` and `0 < sin c <= 1` there. So the integer is at least 1, and `2 n! <= π (π a)^n` for every `n` (`nv_bound`), which fails for large `n`, as `(π a)^n / n!` tends to 0 (Stdlib's `cv_speed_pow_fact`).

`pow_correct` in [`Pow.v`](theories/Pow.v) shows that `Interval::pow`, which computes its bounds with MPFR, returns the tightest interval that contains the image of the box `x × y` under IEEE 1788's `pow`, and `dec_pow_correct` that `DecInterval::pow` decorates it as IEEE 1788 asks, with the hypothesis `com_common` for both arguments. `powi_correct` and `dec_powi_correct` in [`Powi.v`](theories/Powi.v) state the same for `Interval::powi` and `DecInterval::powi`, which are IEEE 1788's `pown`, with `com_common` only for an exponent that is not above 0:

```coq
Theorem pow_correct :
  forall emin emax m first_min first_max x y,
  range_ok emin emax -> get_d_ok emin emax m -> pow_ok emin emax m ->
  valid x -> valid y ->
  is_tightest (image2_set pow_dom Rpow x y) (Interval.pow m first_min first_max x y).

Theorem dec_pow_correct :
  forall emin emax m first_min first_max x y,
  range_ok emin emax -> get_d_ok emin emax m -> pow_ok emin emax m ->
  valid (itv x) -> valid (itv y) -> com_common x -> com_common y ->
  let z := DecInterval.pow m first_min first_max x y in
  if DecInterval.is_nai x || DecInterval.is_nai y then z = DecInterval.NAI
  else
    is_tightest (image2_set pow_dom Rpow (itv x) (itv y)) (itv z) /\
    exists d, strongest2_set pow_dom d Rpow (itv x) (itv y) (itv z) /\
      dec z = dec_min d (dec_min (dec x) (dec y)).

Theorem powi_correct :
  forall emin emax m n x,
  range_ok emin emax -> get_d_ok emin emax m -> pown_ok emin emax m -> is_i32 n ->
  valid x ->
  exists y, Interval.powi m n x = Some y /\
    is_tightest (image_dom (pown_dom n) (fun r => powerRZ r n) x) y.

Theorem dec_powi_correct :
  forall emin emax m n x,
  range_ok emin emax -> get_d_ok emin emax m -> pown_ok emin emax m -> is_i32 n ->
  valid (itv x) -> (0 < n)%Z \/ com_common x ->
  exists y, DecInterval.powi m n x = Some y /\
    if DecInterval.is_nai x then y = DecInterval.NAI
    else
      is_tightest (image_dom (pown_dom n) (fun r => powerRZ r n) (itv x)) (itv y) /\
      exists d, strongest_dom (pown_dom n) d (fun r => powerRZ r n) (itv x) (itv y) /\
        dec y = dec_min d (dec x).
```

For `pow`, `x` is `self` and `y` is `rhs`. `Rpow r t` in `Elementary.v` is `exp(t ln r)`, Stdlib's `Rpower r t`, for `r > 0`, and 0 for `r = 0`, and its domain `pow_dom` holds the points `(r, t)` with `r > 0`, and those with `r = 0` and `t > 0`, as in Table 9.1 of the draft P1788/D9.5. That domain is not a product of two sets of reals, so the image and the decorations are `image2_set`, `p2_set` and `strongest2_set` of `Ieee1788.v`, which take any set of points of R² as the domain (see "What is assumed"). `pow_ok` in `Mpfr.v` is what the theorems assume of `mpfr_pow`. `first_min` and `first_max` say which zero `f64::min` and `f64::max` in `Interval::pow` return if both of their arguments are zeros, as `first` does for `cosh`, and the theorems hold for all four pairs of values. For `powi`, `x` is `self`, and `rhs` is the integer `n`, which `is_i32 n` says an `i32` holds. The point function is Stdlib's `powerRZ r n`: `r` multiplied by itself `n` times for `n > 0`, 1 for `n = 0`, even at `r = 0`, and `1 / r^(-n)` for `n < 0`, with the domain `pown_dom n`, all of R for `n >= 0` and the reals other than 0 for `n < 0`. Table 9.1 makes `pown(x, p)` a family of functions of one real, one for each integer `p`, so `powi` is stated with `image_dom` and `strongest_dom`, as for `sqrt`. `pown_ok` is what the theorems assume of `mpfr_pow_si`. `Some` means that the `transmute` in `classify`, which `Interval::abs` calls for an even `n`, gets the discriminant of a variant.

`Interval::pow` intersects `x` with `[0, +∞]`, as no point of `pow_dom` has `r < 0`, so the image is that of the box with the intersection in place of `x` (`pw_image_inter` in [`PowCases.v`](theories/PowCases.v)), and returns the empty interval if the intersection or `y` is empty. Otherwise, for the intersection `[a, b]` and `y = [c, d]`, `a` is +0 or above 0, never -0 (`pw_xok_inter`), so `pow_ru(a, c)` is never `pow(-0, c)`, which is -∞ for a negative odd integer `c`. For `r > 0`, `r^t` decreases in `r` if `t <= 0` and increases in `r` if `t > 0`, and it decreases in `t` if `r <= 1` and increases in `t` if `r >= 1` ([`PowReal.v`](theories/PowReal.v)). The code splits on the signs of `c` and `d` and on where `[a, b]` is relative to 1, and in each case the bounds of the image are at the corners of the box that it picks:

- `d <= 0` ([`PowNeg.v`](theories/PowNeg.v)): the domain asks for `r > 0`, so the image is empty if `b` is 0. Otherwise the lower and upper bounds are at `(b, d)` and `(a, c)` if `b < 1`, at `(b, c)` and `(a, d)` if `a > 1`, and at `(b, c)` and `(a, c)` otherwise.
- `c > 0` ([`PowPos.v`](theories/PowPos.v)): every point of the box with `r >= 0` is in the domain. The bounds are at `(a, d)` and `(b, c)` if `b < 1`, at `(a, c)` and `(b, d)` if `a > 1`, and at `(a, d)` and `(b, d)` otherwise. `b` may be -0 here, if `x` is `[.., -0]`, and `pow_ru(-0, c)` is then a zero (`pow_fin` in `PowMpfr.v`).
- `c <= 0 < d` ([`PowMix.v`](theories/PowMix.v)): if `b` is 0, the only points of the domain in the box are `(0, t)` with `t > 0`, where `pow` is 0, and the result is `Interval::zero()`. Otherwise the reals of `[a, b]` at most 1 have their least value at `(a, d)` and their largest at `(a, c)`, and those at least 1 at `(b, c)` and `(b, d)`, so the lower bound is `min(pow_rd(a, d), pow_rd(b, c))` ([`PowMixLo.v`](theories/PowMixLo.v)) and the upper bound `max(pow_ru(a, c), pow_ru(b, d))` ([`PowMixHi.v`](theories/PowMixHi.v)). If `a > 1`, `[a, b]` has no real at most 1, but the two bounds are still those of the image, as the corners for the reals at least 1 give both: `pow_rd(a, d)` is at least 1, so at least `pow_rd(b, c)`, and `pow_ru(a, c)` is at most 1, so at most `pow_ru(b, d)`. The same holds the other way round if `b < 1`.

At a corner with `a = 0` and an exponent at most 0, or with `b = +∞`, `c = -∞` or `d = +∞`, the bound of the image is a limit, or there is none, and `pow_rd` and `pow_ru` give it by the special values that `pow_ok` assumes, such as `pow(+0, t) = +∞` for `t < 0`, and `pow(r, +∞)`, a zero for `0 < r < 1`. A corner `(0, t)` with a finite `t > 0` is a point of `pow_dom`, where `pow` is 0, which `pow_fin` gives. As for `exp`, these bounds need not be values of `pow`, as `pow([2, +∞], [-1, -1])` is `[0, 0.5]`, so the lanes are those of least upper bounds (`tightest_lanes_lub` in `DivTight.v`), which `pl_lo`, `pl_hi`, `pl_lo_fmin` and `pl_hi_fmax` in [`PowMpfr.v`](theories/PowMpfr.v) give from the corners.

`Interval::powi` splits on the sign and the parity of `n`, with the facts about `powerRZ` in [`PownReal.v`](theories/PownReal.v). For `n < 0`, the image of `[0, 0]` is empty (`pwi_neg_zero`). For an even `n`, `r^n` depends only on `|r|`, so the code takes `abs` of `x` (`abs_correct`), and `r^n` decreases in `|r|` for `n < 0` (`pwi_neg_even`) and increases for `n >= 0` (`pwi_pos_even`). For an odd `n < 0`, an `x` with reals on both sides of 0 gives `ENTIRE`, as `r^n` has no bound on either side of 0 (`pwi_neg_odd_entire`), and otherwise `r^n` decreases on the side of 0 that `x` is on, and the code makes a lower bound 0 into +0 and an upper bound 0 into -0, so that `pown_ru(+0, n)` is +∞ and `pown_rd(-0, n)` is -∞ (`pwi_neg_odd`). For an odd `n > 0`, `r^n` increases on all of R (`pwi_pos_odd`).

`Rpow_continuous` in `PowReal.v` shows that `pow` is continuous at each point of `pow_dom`, relative to it, including the points `(0, t)` with `t > 0`, as IEEE 1788 counts it, and `pown_cont` in `PownReal.v` that `pown(x, n)` is continuous at each point of `pown_dom n`. `elem_dec2_impl_correct` in [`Dom2.v`](theories/Dom2.v) does for `impl_dec2!` what `elem_dec_impl_correct` does for the second arm of `impl_dec!`, for any function of two reals on any domain, and for an interval function that returns any of the four decorations, if it is the one that `dec_holds` describes (see `atan2` below). It applies `elem2_set_dec_correct` in `DecBinary.v`, the generic theorem about the decorations of a function of two reals: `set_dec` of the tightest interval that contains the image, with the minimum of the decorations of `x` and `y` and the decoration that `dec_holds` describes, returns the tightest interval, decorated with the minimum of the strongest decoration and those of `x` and `y`, if a box of two `com` intervals is bounded when that decoration is `com` and the result is common. It gives `dec_pow_correct` and `dec_atan2_correct`. `DecInterval::pow` passes to `set_dec` the minimum of the decorations of `x` and `y` and of the one that `Interval::pow_impl` returns, which is `trv` if the intersection or `y` is empty, and otherwise `com` if `x` is in the interior of `[0, +∞]`, or, for `c > 0`, a subset of it, and `trv` if not (`pw_dec_cases` in `Pow.v`). That is the decoration that `dec_holds` describes: for `trv`, `pw_dec_cases` shows that the box is empty or has a point outside `pow_dom`, so `def` does not hold, and for `com`, that the box is a nonempty subset of `pow_dom`, at each point of which `pow` is continuous relative to `pow_dom` (`Rpow_continuous`), so relative to the box too (`d2_continue_in2_sub`), and `dac` holds. The strongest decoration is `trv` if the box is empty or has a point outside `pow_dom`, which, for a nonempty box, is when `x` has a real below 0, or has the real 0 and `y` a real at most 0, `com` if the box is a bounded subset of `pow_dom` and the result is common, and `dac` otherwise. As for `atan`, a common result can come from an unbounded argument: `pow([2, +∞], [-1, -0.5])` is `[0, RU(2^-0.5)]`, and `pow([2, 3], [-∞, -1])` is `[0, 0.5]`. So `dec_pow_correct` has the `com_common` hypothesis for both arguments. `DecInterval::powi` passes to `set_dec` the minimum of the decoration of `x` and of `trv` if `x` is empty, or if `n < 0` and 0 is in `x`, and `com` otherwise, so `dec_powi_correct` follows from `elem_set_dec_trv_com` in `ElemDec.v`. For `n > 0`, an unbounded `x` gives an unbounded result, but for `n <= 0`, a common result can come from an unbounded `x`: `x^0` is `[1, 1]` for every nonempty `x`, and `[1, +∞]` to the power -2 is `[0, 1]`. So `dec_powi_correct` has the `com_common` hypothesis for `n <= 0`.

`atan2_correct` in [`Atan2.v`](theories/Atan2.v) shows that `Interval::atan2`, which computes its bounds with MPFR, returns the tightest interval that contains the image of the box `y × x` under IEEE 1788's `atan2`, and `dec_atan2_correct` that `DecInterval::atan2` decorates it as IEEE 1788 asks, with the hypothesis `com_common` for both arguments:

```coq
Theorem atan2_correct :
  forall emin emax m y x,
  range_ok emin emax -> get_d_ok emin emax m -> atan2_ok emin emax m ->
  valid y -> valid x ->
  exists z, Interval.atan2 m y x = Some z /\
    is_tightest (image2_set atan2_dom Ratan2 y x) z.

Theorem dec_atan2_correct :
  forall emin emax m y x,
  range_ok emin emax -> get_d_ok emin emax m -> atan2_ok emin emax m ->
  valid (itv y) -> valid (itv x) -> com_common y -> com_common x ->
  exists w, DecInterval.atan2 m y x = Some w /\
    if DecInterval.is_nai y || DecInterval.is_nai x then w = DecInterval.NAI
    else
      is_tightest (image2_set atan2_dom Ratan2 (itv y) (itv x)) (itv w) /\
      exists d, strongest2_set atan2_dom d Ratan2 (itv y) (itv x) (itv w) /\
        dec w = dec_min d (dec_min (dec y) (dec x)).
```

`y` is `self`, the interval of the `y` coordinates, and `x` is `rhs`, that of the `x` coordinates, as in IEEE 1788's `atan2(y, x)`. `Ratan2 r t` in `Elementary.v` is `atan(r / t)` for `t > 0`, that plus π for `t < 0` and `r >= 0`, and minus π for `t < 0` and `r < 0`, and π/2 or -π/2 by the sign of `r` for `t = 0`, which is also the formula of the `mpfr_atan2` entry of the MPFR manual. Its domain `atan2_dom` is R² without `(0, 0)`, as in Table 9.1 of the draft P1788/D9.5, which defines `atan2(y, x)` as the principal value of the argument (polar angle) of `(x, y)`. `Ratan2_range`, `Ratan2_polar` and `Ratan2_polar_unique` in [`AtanReal.v`](theories/AtanReal.v) show that `Ratan2 r t` is that angle: for a point of `atan2_dom`, it is the only `θ` in `(-π, π]` with `t = ρ cos θ` and `r = ρ sin θ`, where `ρ = sqrt(r² + t²)`. `atan2_dom` is not a product, so the theorems are stated with `image2_set` and `strongest2_set`, as for `pow`. `atan2_ok` in `Mpfr.v` is what the theorems assume of `mpfr_atan2`. `Some` means that the `transmute`s in `classify` and `classify2` get the discriminant of a variant.

`Interval::atan2_impl` matches on `x.classify2(y)`, the class of `x` first, and `atan2_correct` applies one lemma to each of the 49 pairs. The image is empty if either side of the box is empty, and for `[0, 0] × [0, 0]`, whose only point is not in the domain (`at2_empty` in [`Atan2Const.v`](theories/Atan2Const.v)). In the 15 other pairs whose box contains `(0, 0)`, and in `N1_M`, `N1_N0` and `N1_Z`, where `x < 0` and 0 is in `y`, so that the box meets the half-line `y = 0`, `x < 0`, the bounds of the image are among 0, ±π/2, π and -π. Each is the value at a point of the box, except -π, which `atan2` approaches just below the half-line (`Ratan2_y_0neg`). The code returns `Interval::zero()`, `Interval::PI`, `Interval::FRAC_PI_2`, `-Interval::FRAC_PI_2`, or an interval whose bounds are 0 or a bound of `Interval::PI` or `Interval::FRAC_PI_2` or its negation, such as `[-PI.sup, PI.sup]` (`at2_full` and the other lemmas of `Atan2Const.v`). `PI_up`, `PI_dn`, `FRAC_PI_2_up` and `FRAC_PI_2_dn` in [`PiTight.v`](theories/PiTight.v) show that the bounds of `Interval::PI` and `Interval::FRAC_PI_2` are the floats next to π and π/2, as their bit patterns differ by one, so that these results are the tightest. In the other 17 pairs, the box lies in a closed quadrant, or in one of the half-planes `x > 0`, `y > 0` and `y < 0`, without `(0, 0)`, and the bounds are `atan2_rd` and `atan2_ru` at the corners of the box that give the greatest lower bound and the least upper bound of the image, by the monotonicity of `atan2` (`Ratan2_dec_x`, `Ratan2_inc_x`, `Ratan2_inc_y`, `Ratan2_dec_y_ge` and `Ratan2_dec_y_lt` in `AtanReal.v`): for `y > 0` it decreases in `x`, for `y < 0` it increases in `x`, for `x > 0` it increases in `y`, and for `x < 0` it decreases in `y` on `y >= 0` and on `y < 0`. The one exception is the upper bound of `N1_P0`, where `x < 0` and `y = [0, d]`, which is `PI.sup`, as the image has its greatest element π, at the points `(0, t)`. [`Atan2Right.v`](theories/Atan2Right.v) proves the cases in the first quadrant and `M_P1` and `P1_M`, [`Atan2Lower.v`](theories/Atan2Lower.v) those in the fourth quadrant and `M_N1`, and [`Atan2Left.v`](theories/Atan2Left.v) those in the second and third quadrants, `N1_P0` among them.

At a corner with an infinity, the bound of the image is a limit, and `atan2_rd` and `atan2_ru` give it by the special values that `atan2_ok` assumes, such as `atan2(y, +∞)`, a zero for a finite `y`, and `atan2(+∞, x) = π/2` rounded for a finite `x` (`at_val` and `atan2_round` in [`Atan2Mpfr.v`](theories/Atan2Mpfr.v)). These bounds need not be values of `atan2`, as the image of `[1, 2] × [1, +∞]` is `(0, atan(2)]`, so the lanes are those of least upper bounds, which `at_hi_lane` and `at_lo_lane` give from the corners, as `pl_hi` and `pl_lo` do for `pow`. MPFR's `atan2` tells +0 from -0, as C99's does (Annex F.9.1.4 of the draft N1256): `atan2(±0, x)` is `±π` for `x < 0`, and `atan2(±0, ±0)` depends on both signs. The code passes neither: a corner with a zero `y` has an `x` above 0 or +∞, where both zeros give 0, a corner with a zero `x` has a `y` other than 0, where MPFR gives `±π/2` by the sign of `y` alone, and no corner has two infinities (`at_args`). For `N1_Z`, where `y` is `[0, 0]` and `x < 0`, the code returns `Interval::PI` without calling MPFR, which would give -π if the bound 0 of `y` is -0.

`Ratan2_cont` in `AtanReal.v` shows that `atan2` is continuous at each point off the closed half-line `y = 0`, `x <= 0`, and `Ratan2_not_cont` that it is not continuous at a point `(0, t)` with `t < 0`, relative to the domain, as it is π there and near -π just below. So a box in the domain can lose `com` to the discontinuity, and not only, as for `pow`, to an unbounded box or result: it is `dac` if it meets the half-line but has no point below it, as `[0, 1] × [-2, -1]` and `[0, 0] × [-2, -1]` do, where the restriction of `atan2` to the box is `atan(y / x) + π`, which is continuous (`Ratan2_cont_upper`), and `def` if the box also has points just below the half-line, as `[-1, 1] × [-2, -1]` has (`Ratan2_not_cont_in2`). `Interval::atan2_impl` returns `trv` for an empty box and for one that contains `(0, 0)`, `dac` for `N1_P0` and `N1_Z`, `def` for `N1_M` and `N1_N0`, and `com` for the boxes off the closed half-line `y = 0`, `x <= 0`. `at2_impl_dec` in `Atan2.v`, from the lemmas of [`Atan2Dec.v`](theories/Atan2Dec.v), shows that each of these is the decoration that `dec_holds` in `DecBinary.v` describes: `trv` if `def` does not hold, `def` if `def` holds and `dac` does not, `dac` if `dac` holds and `atan2` is not continuous relative to the domain at some point of the box, and `com` if `dac` holds and `atan2` is continuous at each. It also shows that `atan2_impl` returns some pair `p`, not `None`, and the model of `DecInterval::atan2` in `Model.v` then returns `Some` of `elem_dec2_impl` of the interval function that returns `p` for every box, so `elem_dec2_impl_correct` gives `dec_atan2_correct` from `atan2_correct` and `at2_impl_dec`. As for `atan` and `pow`, a common result can come from an unbounded argument, as for `[1, 2] × [1, +∞]` above, which `atan2_impl` decorates `com`. So `dec_atan2_correct` has the `com_common` hypothesis for both arguments.

`from_dec_ninterval_correct` in [`ParseDec.v`](theories/ParseDec.v) shows that `From<DecNInterval> for DecInterval`, which `FromStr` for `DecInterval` calls, does not panic and returns the tightest interval that contains the reals that a decorated interval literal denotes, decorated as IEEE 1788's textToInterval asks, and `from_ninterval_correct` that `From<NInterval> for Interval`, which `FromStr` for `Interval` calls, does not panic and returns that interval. `Model.v` writes a panic as `None`, and `a` and `b` are the bounds of the literal converted to `f64`s:

```coq
Theorem from_dec_ninterval_correct :
  forall x, dec_ninterval_ok x ->
  exists a b y,
  number_to_f64 (ninf (nx x)) Inf = Some a /\
  number_to_f64 (nsup (nx x)) Sup = Some b /\
  DecInterval.from_dec_ninterval x = Some y /\
  let overflow :=
    bounded (ninterval_set (nx x)) /\ ~ bounded (fun r => mem r (itv y)) in
  (if dec_eqb (nd x) Ill then y = DecInterval.NAI
   else is_tightest (ninterval_set (nx x)) (itv y)) /\
  (nd x = Com /\ overflow -> dec y = Dac) /\
  (~ (nd x = Com /\ overflow) -> dec y = nd x) /\
  (nd x = Com -> (overflow <-> f64_overflow a || f64_overflow b = true)).

Theorem from_ninterval_correct :
  forall x, ninterval_ok x ->
  exists y, DecInterval.from_ninterval x = Some y /\ is_tightest (ninterval_set x) y.
```

`FromStr` works in two steps. The `nom` grammar of `src/parse.rs`, which is not proved apart from the step that decorates a literal (see "What is not proved yet"), reads the literal and returns its bounds, exact rationals (`Rat`s) or the infinities, as an `NInterval`, with its decoration as a `DecNInterval` `x`, whose fields are `nx x` and `nd x`. Then `From<DecNInterval>` rounds the lower bound down and the upper bound up to `f64`s with `number_to_f64`, and decorates the result with `set_dec`. `ninterval_set x` in `Ieee1788.v` is the set of reals that `x` denotes, those at or above its lower bound and at or below its upper bound, and `ninterval_ok x` in `Model.v` is what the grammar is checked by reading to return: `NInterval::EMPTY`, or bounds in order whose lower bound is not +∞ and whose upper bound is not -∞. Without it, `[q, p]` for two rationals `p < q` between the same two adjacent floats would give a nonempty interval for an empty set. It also asks `number_ok` of both bounds, which bounds the sizes and the exponents of a rational (`rat_ok`, see "What is assumed"), and under which the conversion of a bound does not panic (`number_to_f64_correct`). `dec_ninterval_ok x` in `Model.v` is `ninterval_ok (nx x)` and what the grammar gives as decorations: `com` only if both bounds are rationals, to `NInterval::EMPTY` only `trv` or `ill`, and `ill` only to `NInterval::EMPTY`. Its first two conjuncts on `nd x` rule out exactly the combinations that §11.4 of the draft P1788/D9.5 and §5.4 of the draft P1788.1/D9.8 call contradictory, `∅dac`, `∅def`, and `xcom` if x is empty or unbounded, as an `NInterval` with `ninterval_ok` whose bounds are not both rationals is empty or unbounded. The third gives `ill` only to NaI, which both drafts identify with `(∅, ill)` (§12.5.1 of P1788/D9.5, §6.1.2 of P1788.1/D9.8), and which is the decorated value of a literal only if the literal is `[nai]` (§12.11.5 of P1788/D9.5, §6.6.3 and §6.7.5 of P1788.1/D9.8). For an `NInterval` `x` with `ninterval_ok`, `ParseDec.v` proves `dec_ninterval_ok` of what the step of the grammar that decorates a literal returns: of `DecNInterval::NAI`, for `[nai]` (`dn_NAI_ok`), of `DecNInterval::new(x)`, for a literal without a decoration (`dn_new_ok`), of `DecNInterval::set_dec(x, d)` for any `d` (`dn_set_dec_ok`), and of what `dn_decorated x d` returns for a literal with a decoration `d`, which is `set_dec(x, d)` if its decoration is `d`, and otherwise nothing, as the literal is rejected (`dn_decorated_ok`). `dn_decorated_ok` also shows that a literal that this step accepts keeps its decoration, and its `NInterval` unless the decoration is `ill`, which `decoration` does not parse. So, given `ninterval_ok`, the step rejects a literal whose own combination is contradictory, which by §12.11.5 of the draft P1788/D9.5 has no decorated value, as it rejects `[1,inf]_com` and `[empty]_dac` (also tested in `src/parse.rs`). That the `nom` combinators pass each literal to this step and return what it returns is checked by reading (see "What is assumed"). For `ill`, the result is NaI. Otherwise it is the tightest interval that contains the reals that the literal denotes, and in every case it is decorated with the decoration of the literal, except `dac` in place of `com` if overflow occurred, "that is, x is bounded and y is unbounded", as §12.12.7 of the draft P1788/D9.5 and §6.7.5 of the draft P1788.1/D9.8 ask, with `ninterval_set (nx x)` as x and the reals of the result as y. A literal that denotes no real is `NInterval::EMPTY` (`ninterval_ok_set`), decorated `trv` or `ill` by `dec_ninterval_ok`, and the result keeps that decoration. For a `com` literal, the last conjunct shows that overflow occurred exactly when the conversion of a bound overflowed. `from_dec_ninterval_set_dec`, which the proof uses, states the decoration as `set_dec` gives it, for any `x` with `ninterval_ok (nx x)`: NaI for `ill`, and otherwise `trv` if the literal denotes no real, and otherwise the decoration of the literal, except `dac` in place of `com` if the result is not common. `From<DecNInterval>` itself lowers the decoration to `dac` when a bound overflowed, which `set_dec` would do anyway, as an overflowed bound is infinite (`overflow_not_common`). `from_ninterval_new` shows, for every `x`, that `From<DecNInterval>` of `DecNInterval::new(x)`, which `FromStr` uses for a literal without a decoration, is `DecInterval::new` of the interval that `From<NInterval>` returns, i.e. newDec of it, as §6.7.5 of the draft P1788.1/D9.8 asks of a bare literal, and panics exactly when that does.

`rational_to_f64_correct` in [`ParseRound.v`](theories/ParseRound.v) shows that `rational_to_f64` does not panic for a `Rat` `x` with `rat_ok`, rounds `rat_R x`, the rational `(-1)^neg * num * 2^e2 * 5^e5 / den` that `x` holds, once to an `f64`, toward -∞ for a lower bound (`Inf`) and toward +∞ for an upper bound (`Sup`), and that its flags `inexact` and `overflow` say whether `x` is not a finite `f64` and whether the result is infinite. It has no hypothesis on MPFR: `mpfr_round` and `with_val` are only definitions. Like every theorem about `src/parse.rs`, it reads each operation of `Nat` as its loop on the limbs of the integers that the `Nat`s hold, which computes the operation on integers (see `mul_z_correct` below and "What is assumed"). `scale_correct` in [`ParseScale.v`](theories/ParseScale.v) shows what `scale`, which `rational_to_f64` calls, returns for a nonzero `x`:

```coq
Theorem rational_to_f64_correct :
  forall x infsup, rat_ok x ->
  exists F, rational_to_f64 x infsup = Some F /\
  mpfr_round f64_format (as_rnd_t infsup) (rat_R x) (with_val (f64_f F)) /\
  (f64_inexact F = false <-> f64_format (rat_R x)) /\
  f64_overflow F = negb (is_finite (f64_f F)).

Theorem scale_correct :
  forall x, rat_ok x -> rat_num x <> 0%Z ->
  exists s, scale x = Some s /\ scaled_ok s (Rabs (rat_R x)).
```

`rational_to_f64` returns +0, exact, for a `Rat` whose numerator is 0. Otherwise it rounds `x` up with `round_up_scaled` of the sign of `x` and of `scale x`, and rounds `x` down as the negation of `-x` rounded up, with the same `scale x`, as `-x` has the magnitude of `x`. `scale` returns `Huge` (`|x| > 2^1024`) or `Tiny` (`0 < |x| < 2^-1074`) from bit lengths where it can, before it computes `5^|e5|`: a positive integer of `k` bits lies in `[2^(k - 1), 2^k)`, and `5^k` lies between `4^k` and `8^k`, so the bit lengths of `num` and `den` and the exponents `e2` and `e5` bound `|x|`. Otherwise it multiplies `5^|e5|` into the numerator or the denominator, returns `Huge` or `Tiny` if the new bit lengths imply it, and else shifts and divides with remainder to `Mid q e sticky`, with `|x| = (q + f) * 2^e`, `2^55 <= q < 2^57`, `-2^20 <= e <= 2^20` and a `0 <= f < 1` that is 0 exactly when `sticky` is false (`scaled_ok`). `round_up_scaled` returns +∞ for a positive and `f64::MIN` for a negative `Huge`, `f64::from_bits(1)`, the least positive subnormal, for a positive and -0 for a negative `Tiny`, and for `Mid` calls `round_up_exact` of `src/simd/generic.rs`, which `round_up_exact_correct` in `RoundUpExact.v` proves (`round_up_scaled_correct`). `inexact` is `up != down`, which is false exactly when `x` is a finite `f64` (`rounds_up_to_eq`), and `overflow` is whether the result is infinite. `number_to_f64_correct` states the same for `number_to_f64` of a `Number` with `number_ok`, which calls `rational_to_f64` for a rational and returns -∞ and +∞ as they are, neither inexact nor overflowed (`number_round` and `number_exact`). `number_neg_correct` shows that `-x` of a `Number`, which the grammar uses for a number with a minus sign, negates the reals that it bounds.

`mul_z_correct` and `div_rem_z_correct` in [`NatOps.v`](theories/NatOps.v) show that `&a * &b` and `a.div_rem(&b)` of `Nat`s compute the product, and the quotient and the remainder, of the integers that they hold:

```coq
Lemma mul_z_correct : forall a b, 0 <= a -> 0 <= b -> NatLimbs.mul_z a b = Some (a * b).

Lemma div_rem_z_correct :
  forall a b, 0 <= a -> 0 < b -> NatLimbs.div_rem_z a b = Some (a / b, a mod b).
```

`Model.v` transcribes the operations of `Nat` that `scale` and the functions of `src/format/digits.rs` call as loops on lists of limbs, in `NatLimbs`, and reads a `Nat` that holds `a` as `NatLimbs.to_limbs a`, the only list of 64-bit limbs with no zero limb at the top that holds `a` (`to_limbs_correct` and `to_limbs_val`). `NatLimbs.mul_z a b` is the value of the limbs that the loop of `*` returns from the limbs of `a` and `b`, and `None` a panic, here the overflow of a `u128` that the theorem rules out. `div_rem_z_zero` shows that `div_rem` by 0 panics. `shl_z_correct`, `bits_z_correct`, `is_zero_z_correct`, `low_u128_z_correct`, `pow5_z_correct`, `add_z_correct`, `from_u64_z_correct` and `digits10_z_correct` do the same for `<<` by a `u64`, `bits`, `is_zero`, `low_u128`, `Nat::pow5` of a `u32`, `+`, `Nat::from_u64` and `digits10`, which gives Flocq's `Zdigits radix10` for a number of fewer than `2^32` digits. Each loop returns limbs with no zero limb at the top, so its result is read in the same way, and the theorems chain. The loops call those of `src/limbs.rs`, which the dyadic kernel below shares, and [`Limbs.v`](theories/Limbs.v), [`KnuthStep.v`](theories/KnuthStep.v) and [`Knuth.v`](theories/Knuth.v) prove each of them. `div_rem_correct` in `Knuth.v` shows that `div_rem`, which divides by one limb, or else by Algorithm D of Knuth (The Art of Computer Programming, vol. 2, 3rd ed., 4.3.1) once it has dropped the zero limbs at the bottom of the divisor, returns the quotient and the remainder:

```coq
Lemma div_rem_correct :
  forall x d,
  limbs_ok x -> limbs_ok d -> Limbs.val d <> 0 ->
  exists q r,
  Limbs.div_rem x d = Some (q, r) /\ limbs_ok q /\ limbs_ok r /\
  length q = length x /\ length r = length d /\
  Limbs.val q = Limbs.val x / Limbs.val d /\ Limbs.val r = Limbs.val x mod Limbs.val d.
```

`limbs_ok x` says that each limb of `x` is a `u64`, and `Limbs.val x` is the number that the limbs hold, the least significant first. `knuth_step_correct` in `KnuthStep.v` proves a step of Algorithm D, D3 to D6: the estimate `qhat` of a limb of the quotient is never too small (Knuth's Theorem A, `est_upper`) and, once the top limb of the divisor is at least `2^63`, at most 2 too large (Theorem B, `est_lower`), so that the loop of D3 ends within the 3 tests that the model allows it, at most 1 too large (`qhat_loop`), and the one addition of the divisor in D6 corrects the rest.

`bounds_tightest` in [`ParseHull.v`](theories/ParseHull.v) shows that `Interval::try_from` of the bounds of an `x` with `ninterval_ok x` rounded down and up, as `number_round` says, or `Interval::EMPTY` where it fails, is the tightest interval that contains `ninterval_set x`, which `ParseDec.v` decorates. `NInterval::EMPTY` has the lower bound +∞, which `try_from` rejects. Otherwise the negation of the lower bound rounded down is the least float or +∞ at or above the negations of the reals of `x`, and the upper bound rounded up the least at or above its reals (`round_dn_lane` and `round_up_lane` in `MpfrRound.v`), or +∞ for an infinite bound, so the lanes are those of least upper bounds (`tightest_lanes_lub` in `DivTight.v`), and `try_from` accepts them.

`try_from_ninterval_exact_correct` in [`ParseExact.v`](theories/ParseExact.v) shows that `Interval::try_from_ninterval_exact`, which `interval_exact!` calls, does not panic for bounds with `number_ok`, and returns `Ok` exactly when both bounds of the literal are exact, i.e. infinities or rationals that are finite `f64`s, and then the interval whose reals are exactly those that the literal denotes, which is the tightest one, as the doc comment of `interval_exact!` says:

```coq
Theorem try_from_ninterval_exact_correct :
  forall x, number_ok (ninf x) -> number_ok (nsup x) ->
  exists z, Interval.try_from_ninterval_exact x = Some z /\
  match z with
  | Some y =>
      number_exact (ninf x) /\ number_exact (nsup x) /\
      is_tightest (ninterval_set x) y /\ (forall r, mem r y <-> ninterval_set x r)
  | None => ~ (number_exact (ninf x) /\ number_exact (nsup x))
  end.
```

`Some (Some y)` is `Ok(y)`, `Some None` the `Err`, and `None` a panic. A bound that is not exact gives `Err`, whatever the other bound. Unlike `from_ninterval_correct`, the theorem needs only `number_ok` of both bounds, not `ninterval_ok`: exact bounds in the wrong order give `Interval::EMPTY`, which holds no real, as the literal then denotes none.

`format_fixed_correct` and `format_sci_correct` in [`FormatDec.v`](theories/FormatDec.v), and `format_hex_exact_correct`, `format_hex_bin_correct` and `format_hex_digits_correct` in [`FormatHex.v`](theories/FormatHex.v), show that `fixed`, `sci` and `hex` in `src/format/digits.rs`, with which `Display`, `LowerExp` and `LowerHex` of intervals and decorated intervals compute the digits of a finite bound `x` other than 0, do not panic, and round `x` toward +∞ if `up` and toward -∞ otherwise, as the digits `d` of its magnitude and an exponent `k` of 10 or 2. `eq x zero = false` is `x != 0.0`, and `p < 2^64` holds for every `usize` (see "What is assumed"):

```coq
Theorem format_fixed_correct :
  forall x p up, is_finite x = true -> eq x zero = false -> 0 <= p < 2^64 ->
  exists d k, format_fixed x p up = Some (d, k) /\ 0 <= d /\ - p <= k <= 0 /\
  cond_Ropp (Bsign x) (IZR d * bpow radix10 k)%R =
  round radix10 (FIX_exp (- p)) (if up then Zceil else Zfloor) (B2R x).

Theorem format_sci_correct :
  forall x p up, is_finite x = true -> eq x zero = false -> 0 <= p < 2^64 ->
  exists d k, format_sci x p up = Some (d, k) /\ 0 <= d /\
  cond_Ropp (Bsign x) (IZR d * bpow radix10 k)%R =
  round radix10 (FLX_exp (p + 1)) (if up then Zceil else Zfloor) (B2R x).

Theorem format_hex_exact_correct :
  forall x up, is_finite x = true -> eq x zero = false ->
  exists d k, format_hex x None up = Some (d, k) /\ 0 <= d /\
  cond_Ropp (Bsign x) (IZR d * bpow radix2 k)%R = B2R x.

Theorem format_hex_bin_correct :
  forall x up, is_finite x = true -> eq x zero = false ->
  exists d k, format_hex x (Some 0) up = Some (d, k) /\ 0 <= d /\
  cond_Ropp (Bsign x) (IZR d * bpow radix2 k)%R =
  round radix2 (FLX_exp 4) (if up then Zceil else Zfloor) (B2R x).

Theorem format_hex_digits_correct :
  forall x p up, is_finite x = true -> eq x zero = false -> 1 <= p < 2^64 ->
  exists d k, format_hex x (Some p) up = Some (d, k) /\ 0 <= d /\
  cond_Ropp (Bsign x) (IZR d * bpow radix2 k)%R =
  round radix16 (FLX_exp (p + 1)) (if up then Zceil else Zfloor) (B2R x).
```

`format_fixed x p up`, for `{:.p}`, and for `{}` with `p = 6`, rounds `x` to a multiple of `10^-p`, and `format_sci x p up`, for `{:.pe}`, and for `{:e}` with `p = 16`, to `p + 1` significant decimal digits. `format_hex x None up`, for `{:x}`, gives `x` exactly, `format_hex x (Some 0) up`, for `{:.0x}`, rounds it to 4 significant bits, and `format_hex x (Some p) up`, for `{:.px}` with `p >= 1`, to `p + 1` significant hexadecimal digits. Flocq's `round` with `Zfloor` gives the largest number of the format at or below `x`, and with `Zceil` the smallest at or above it, so each result is the tightest in its direction. `radix10` and `radix16` are defined in `Model.v`. `src/format.rs` writes the text of 0 and of the infinities without these functions, and the text of the other bounds from `d` and `k`. That text, and that `src/format.rs` rounds the lower bound toward -∞ and the upper bound toward +∞, are not proved (see "What is not proved yet"). The tests in `src/format/diff_tests.rs` compare it with the text of `mpfr_asprintf` with `%RDf` and `%RUf`, `%RDe` and `%RUe`, and `%RDa` and `%RUa`, and the precision, which inari printed before. In these tests it is the same, except where MPFR's `%.0RUa` of a positive number and `%.0RDa` of a negative one, of at most 4 significant bits, add 1 to the digit, though the number is exact, which `format_hex_bin_correct` rules out (see `src/format.rs`). The proofs build on [`FormatCut.v`](theories/FormatCut.v): `format_cut_dec_correct` and `format_cut_bin_correct` show that `cut_dec` and `cut_bin`, which divide `n * 10^s` or `m * 2^e` with remainder by a power of the radix, round it toward zero, or away from it if their argument `away` is true, to a multiple of that power, and `round_sign` that rounding `x` toward -∞ or +∞ is rounding its magnitude toward zero or away from it, by the sign of `x` and the direction, as `format_away` tells.

`round_correct` in [`DyRound.v`](theories/DyRound.v) shows that `round`, with which each operation of the dyadic kernel of `src/dyadic.rs` rounds its exact result, returns the number that its limbs `x`, its exponent `e` and its sticky bit stand for, rounded toward +∞ if `up` and toward -∞ otherwise to `dprec n = 64 n` bits (`dround`), as a normalized `Dyadic` of `n` limbs (`dy_ok`). `add_correct` in [`DyOps.v`](theories/DyOps.v) shows, from it, that `a.add(&b, up)` returns `a + b` rounded in that way:

```coq
Lemma round_correct :
  forall (n : nat) (neg : bool) (x : list Z) (e : Z) (sticky up : bool) (t : R),
  level_ok n -> limbs_ok x ->
  (if sticky then (0 < t < 1)%R /\ 2 ^ (dprec n - 1) <= Limbs.val x else t = 0%R) ->
  (Limbs.val x = 0 \/ - 2 ^ 63 <= e + nat_bits (Limbs.val x) - dprec n < 2 ^ 63 - 1) ->
  exists d,
  Dyadic.round n neg x e sticky up = Some d /\ dy_ok n d /\
  Dyadic.dy_R d = dround n up (cond_Ropp neg ((IZR (Limbs.val x) + t) * bpow radix2 e)).

Lemma add_correct :
  forall n a b up,
  level_ok n -> dy_ok n a -> dy_ok n b -> exp_small a -> exp_small b ->
  exists d,
  Dyadic.add n a b up = Some d /\ dy_ok n d /\
  Dyadic.dy_R d = dround n up (Dyadic.dy_R a + Dyadic.dy_R b).
```

`Dyadic.dy_R` is the value `(-1)^neg * m * 2^exp` of a `Dyadic`, and `dround n` is Flocq's `round` with `FLX_exp (dprec n)`, and `Zceil` or `Zfloor`. `level_ok n` is `1 <= n <= 64`, the precisions of Ziv's strategy up to its cap of 4,096 bits. `dy_ok n a` says that `a` has `n` limbs and an `i64` exponent, and is 0 as `Dyadic::ZERO` or has the top bit of its mantissa set. `exp_small` bounds the exponent by `2^61` in magnitude, far beyond the range of `f64`, so that the exponent of the result stays in the range of `i64`, outside of which the Rust code panics. `sub_correct`, `mul_correct`, `mul_u64_correct`, `div_u64_correct`, `div_correct` and `sqrt_correct` state the same of `a - b`, `a * b`, `a * k` and `a / k` for a `u64` `k`, `a / b` and the square root, and `div_u64_zero`, `div_zero` and `sqrt_neg` show that a division by 0 and the square root of a negative number panic, as the Rust comments say. Each proof shows that the window of limbs that the operation passes to `round`, with its sticky bit, stands for its exact result as `round_correct` asks, and the square root uses `isqrt_correct` in [`DyIsqrt.v`](theories/DyIsqrt.v): `isqrt`, Newton's iteration on limbs, returns the integer square root of the window. `from_f64_correct` in [`DyConv.v`](theories/DyConv.v) shows that `Dyadic::from_f64` of a finite `f64` is exact, and `to_f64_correct` that `to_f64` rounds a `Dyadic` to an `f64` as `mpfr_round` says, overflow included:

```coq
Lemma to_f64_correct :
  forall n x up,
  level_ok n -> dy_ok n x ->
  exists v,
  Dyadic.to_f64 n x up = Some v /\
  mpfr_round f64_format (if up then RNDU else RNDD) (Dyadic.dy_R x) (with_val v).
```

[`DyDriver.v`](theories/DyDriver.v) shows that each operation on enclosures `Encl` returns an enclosure of every result of the operation on points of its operands, with the extreme exact results of the operation on the bounds, rounded outward, as its bounds (`encl_add_correct` and the others below), and that Ziv's strategy, `ziv`, when it returns, returns the real number `r` that every level encloses rounded to an `f64` as `mpfr_round` says (`ziv_correct`). That is what `point_correct` in `Contract.v` asks of the fields of a `point`, for `r = f (B2R x)`. `ev n` is the enclosure that an evaluation computes at `n` limbs, `None` where it panics, and `ziv ev up start` tries the levels 1, 2, 4, ..., 64 from `start` on, until the two bounds of the enclosure round to the same `f64`, and panics, `None`, after the last:

```coq
Lemma encl_add_correct :
  forall n a b,
  level_ok n -> encl_ok n a -> encl_ok n b -> encl_small a -> encl_small b ->
  exists c,
  Dyadic.encl_add n a b = Some c /\ encl_ok n c /\
  lo_R c = dround n false (lo_R a + lo_R b) /\ hi_R c = dround n true (hi_R a + hi_R b) /\
  forall x y, encl_has a x -> encl_has b y -> encl_has c (x + y).

Lemma ziv_correct :
  forall ev up start r v,
  (forall n a, level_ok n -> ev n = Some a -> encl_ok n a /\ encl_has a r) ->
  Dyadic.ziv ev up start = Some v ->
  mpfr_round f64_format (if up then RNDU else RNDD) r (with_val v).

Lemma ziv_at_passes :
  forall r,
  ~ f64_format r ->
  exists delta,
  (0 < delta)%R /\
  forall n up ev a,
  level_ok n -> ev n = Some a -> encl_ok n a -> encl_has a r -> (hi_R a - lo_R a < delta)%R ->
  exists v, Dyadic.ziv_at ev n up = Some (Some v).
```

`encl_has a r` says that the bounds of `a` enclose `r`, and `ziv_at ev n up`, one level, returns `Some (Some v)` when it passes. `encl_sub_correct`, `encl_mul_correct`, `encl_div_correct`, `encl_sqrt_correct`, `encl_mul_u64_correct`, `encl_div_u64_correct`, `encl_shl_correct`, `encl_point_correct` and `encl_neg_correct` state the same as `encl_add_correct` for the other operations, `encl_mul_correct` and `encl_div_correct` with the least and the greatest of the four products or quotients of the bounds, for a divisor whose enclosure does not contain 0 (`encl_div_zero`: otherwise it panics), `encl_sqrt_correct` for the numbers of the enclosure that are not negative, with the lower bound taken as 0 if it is negative, and for an upper bound that is not negative (`encl_sqrt_neg`: otherwise it panics), and `encl_shl_correct` for a shift of at most `2^62` in magnitude. `ziv_at_passes` shows that a level passes, for a real number that is not an `f64`, once its enclosure is narrower than a width that depends only on that number. It does not show that `ziv` returns (see "What is not proved yet"). `dround_width` and `encl_round_width` in `DyRound.v` bound the width that rounding adds, and `encl_add_width` and `encl_sub_width` the width of a sum and a difference of enclosures:

```coq
Lemma dround_width :
  forall n r,
  level_ok n -> (dround n true r - dround n false r <= Rabs r * bpow radix2 (1 - dprec n))%R.

Lemma encl_add_width :
  forall n a b c,
  level_ok n -> encl_ok n a -> encl_ok n b -> encl_small a -> encl_small b ->
  Dyadic.encl_add n a b = Some c ->
  (hi_R c - lo_R c <=
   (hi_R a - lo_R a) + (hi_R b - lo_R b)
   + (Rabs (lo_R a + lo_R b) + Rabs (hi_R a + hi_R b)) * bpow radix2 (1 - dprec n))%R.
```

The tests in `src/dyadic/tests.rs` compare the arithmetic operations and `to_f64`, in both directions and at 1, 2, 3, 4, 8, 16, 32 and 64 limbs, with MPFR at the same precision, and Ziv's strategy, on a few evaluations built from these operations, with MPFR's rounding to `f64`.

`exp_dir_correct` in [`DyExp.v`](theories/DyExp.v) shows that `exp(x, up)` of `src/dyadic/exp.rs`, which computes e^x with the kernel, returns, at a finite `x` and where it returns rather than panics, e^x rounded toward +∞ if `up` and toward -∞ otherwise, as `mpfr_round` says. `exp2_dir_correct` and `exp10_dir_correct` in `DyExp.v`, and `sinh_dir_correct`, `cosh_dir_correct` and `tanh_dir_correct` in [`DyHyp.v`](theories/DyHyp.v), state the same of `exp2`, `exp10`, `sinh`, `cosh` and `tanh`, with `Rexp2`, `Rexp10`, `sinh`, `cosh` and `tanh` in place of `exp`. `DyadicExp.exp_dir x up` in `Model.v` is `exp(x, up)`, with `None` where the Rust panics. Each function first settles some arguments by rules: those beyond a threshold past which the results overflow or are below 2^-1075 in magnitude, such as `x >= 710` and `x <= -746` for e^x, those of magnitude below 2^-54 for e^x and 2^x, 2^-56 for 10^x and 2^-26 for the hyperbolic functions, whose results are within an ulp of 1 or of `x`, the integers from 1 to 22 for 10^x, whose results are `f64`s, and `tanh x` for `|x| >= 19`, which is within 2^-53 of 1 or -1. Ziv's strategy could not settle an argument whose result is an `f64`, such as 0, unless an enclosure has that `f64` as its bound in the direction of the rounding, as the exact enclosures of 2^x at an integer `x` do, since the two bounds of any other enclosure round to different `f64`s. Each rule has a lemma, such as `exp_overflow_rule` in [`DyExpRules.v`](theories/DyExpRules.v), that it returns the rounding in its direction. The other arguments go to `ziv_correct`, with the enclosures that `ev_exp_correct` and the others show hold the result at each level. `ln2_correct` in [`DyConst.v`](theories/DyConst.v) shows that `ln2` splits ln 2 into a `Dyadic` `h` and an enclosure of `ln 2 - h`, with which the reductions write e^x as `2^k e^-y` for `y = k ln 2 - x`, and `ln10_correct` the same of ln 10, for 10^x:

```coq
Theorem exp_dir_correct :
  forall x up v,
  is_finite x = true -> DyadicExp.exp_dir x up = Some v ->
  mpfr_round f64_format (if up then RNDU else RNDD) (exp (B2R x)) (with_val v).

Lemma ln2_correct :
  forall n h l,
  level_ok n -> DyadicConsts.ln2 n = Some (h, l) ->
  dy_ok n h /\ - 2 ^ 13 <= Dyadic.dy_exp h <= 2 ^ 13 /\
  encl_ok n l /\ encl_le (2 ^ 14) l /\ encl_has l (ln 2 - Dyadic.dy_R h).
```

`encl_le E a`, in [`DyGrow.v`](theories/DyGrow.v), says that the exponents of both bounds of `a` are at most `E` in magnitude. `DyGrow.v` bounds how much each operation on enclosures can grow them, so that every enclosure of the evaluations has `encl_small`, which the theorems of the operations ask of their operands. The tables of ln 2 and ln 10 in `src/dyadic/consts.rs` are their first 4,224 bits, and `DyConst.v` shows it with `vm_compute` of sums of the series of atanh, `ln 2 = 2 atanh(1/3)` and `ln 10 = 3 ln 2 + 2 atanh(1/9)`, at 4,288 bits, whose remainders `atanh_sum_bounds` in [`AtanhSeries.v`](theories/AtanhSeries.v) bounds. `k_exp_c` in [`DyPoint.v`](theories/DyPoint.v) shows that `k_exp`, the kernel's `exp` as the field of a record `point` (see "What is assumed"), satisfies `exp_c` of `Contract.v`, so that `kernel_exp_correct` gives `exp_correct` for a record `point` whose `p_exp` is `k_exp`, and `k_exp2_c` to `k_tanh_c` and `kernel_exp2_correct` to `kernel_tanh_correct` the same for the five others:

```coq
Theorem k_exp_c : exp_c k_exp.

Theorem kernel_exp_correct :
  forall p x, valid x -> is_tightest (image_dom real_dom exp x) (Interval.exp (kernel_point p) x).
```

The tests in `src/dyadic/exp/tests.rs` take these six functions, in both directions, at random arguments, at each rule's threshold and the arguments next to it, where the reductions change their `k`, at the integers, at the arguments of MPFR's own tests of these functions, and at arguments whose results are close to an `f64`. They check them against the roundings that bounds on the functions determine, where these do, and, with the `gmp` feature, against those of `src/mpfr_calls/mpfr.rs`, which call MPFR. Those in `src/dyadic/exp/tests/levels.rs` force each level from 1 to 64 limbs at a few arguments, check that each level either fails or gives the rounding, and, with `gmp`, that each enclosure holds the value that MPFR computes at 8,192 bits and that each doubling of the precision at least halves its width.

`ln_dir_correct` in [`DyLog.v`](theories/DyLog.v) shows that `ln(x, up)` of `src/dyadic/log.rs`, which computes ln x with the kernel, returns, at a finite `x > 0` and where it returns rather than panics, ln x rounded toward +∞ if `up` and toward -∞ otherwise, as `mpfr_round` says. `log2_dir_correct` and `log10_dir_correct` in `DyLog.v`, and `asinh_dir_correct`, `acosh_dir_correct` and `atanh_dir_correct` in [`DyLogHyp.v`](theories/DyLogHyp.v), state the same of `log2`, `log10`, `asinh`, `acosh` and `atanh`, with `Rlog2`, `Rlog10`, `arcsinh`, `Racosh` and `Ratanh` in place of `ln`, at a finite `x` in `log_dom` for `log2` and `log10`, at every finite `x` for `asinh`, and at a finite `x` in `acosh_dom` and in `atanh_dom` for `acosh` and `atanh`. `DyadicLog.ln_dir x up` in `Model.v` is `ln(x, up)`, with `None` where the Rust panics. Each function first settles some arguments by rules: NaN, the infinities, the arguments outside its domain, ±0 for `ln`, `log2` and `log10` and ±1 for `atanh`, whose results are infinite, those of magnitude below 2^-26 for `asinh` and `atanh`, whose results are within an ulp of `x`, and the `f64`s `1e0` to `1e22` for `log10`, whose results are the integers from 0 to 22. At the finite arguments in the domains, the theorems above meet two kinds of these rules: that of `log10` at `1e0` to `1e22`, whose lemma is `log10_pow10_rule` in [`DyLogRules.v`](theories/DyLogRules.v), and those of `asinh` and `atanh` below 2^-26, whose lemmas are `asinh_tiny_rule` and `atanh_tiny_rule` in [`DyLogTiny.v`](theories/DyLogTiny.v). Each lemma shows that its rule returns the rounding in its direction. `log_rules_finite_pos` shows that the rules of `ln`, `log2` and `log10` settle no finite `x > 0`. `k_ln_c` and the others below check by computation the infinite results that `Contract.v` asks for at ±0, ±1 and the infinities. Ziv's strategy settles 1 for `ln` and `acosh` and 2^k for `log2`, whose results are `f64`s, because the evaluations give exact enclosures there, as the tests check. The arguments that no rule settles go to `ziv_correct`, with the enclosures that `ev_ln_correct` and the others show hold the result at each level. These build on [`DyLogCore.v`](theories/DyLogCore.v). `reduce_correct` shows that where `reduce` returns at a `Dyadic` `d`, `d` is positive, and `reduce` gives an integer `e` with `|e| <= 2048` and an enclosure of `ln d - e ln 2`, negated if its flag `less` is set. `reduce` computes it as `2 atanh(|m - 1| / (m + 1))` for `m = d / 2^e` (`ln_atanh` and `ln_atanh_inv`). `atanh_series_correct` shows that `atanh_series`, where it returns, encloses atanh of every point of its argument, by Horner's scheme on the scaled remainders of the series of atanh (`atanh_tail` and `atanh_horner`, from `atanh_sum_bounds`). `ln_dy_correct` shows that `ln_dy`, which adds `e ln 2` with the enclosures of `ln2_correct`, encloses `ln d`. `ln_encl_correct` shows that `ln_encl` encloses ln of every point of an enclosure of positive numbers, which `asinh`, `acosh` and `atanh` take where they do not take the series:

```coq
Theorem ln_dir_correct :
  forall x up v,
  is_finite x = true -> log_dom (B2R x) -> DyadicLog.ln_dir x up = Some v ->
  mpfr_round f64_format (if up then RNDU else RNDD) (ln (B2R x)) (with_val v).

Lemma atanh_series_correct :
  forall n u c E uv,
  level_ok n -> encl_ok n u -> encl_le E u -> 0 <= E <= 2 ^ 30 -> encl_has u uv ->
  DyadicLog.atanh_series n u = Some c ->
  encl_ok n c /\ encl_le (2 ^ 43) c /\ encl_has c (Ratanh uv).
```

`k_ln_c` to `k_atanh_c` in `DyPoint.v` show that `k_ln`, `k_log2`, `k_log10`, `k_asinh`, `k_acosh` and `k_atanh`, the kernel's six functions as fields of a record `point`, satisfy `ln_c`, `log2_c`, `log10_c`, `asinh_c`, `acosh_c` and `atanh_c` of `Contract.v`, with `kernel_field_correct_dom` for the domains other than `real_dom`, so that `kernel_ln_correct` to `kernel_atanh_correct` give `ln_correct` to `atanh_correct` for a record `point` whose fields are these:

```coq
Theorem k_ln_c : ln_c k_ln.

Theorem kernel_ln_correct :
  forall p x, valid x -> is_tightest (image_dom log_dom ln x) (Interval.ln (kernel_point p) x).
```

The tests in `src/dyadic/log/tests.rs` take these six functions, in both directions, at the special values, at 2^k, at `1e0` to `1e22` and at 1, at each rule's threshold and the arguments next to it, where the reductions change their `e` or their branch, at arguments whose results are close to an `f64`, at the hardest arguments that a search found, and at random arguments, uniform in intervals and log-uniform over the exponents. They check them against the roundings that bounds on the functions determine, where these do, and, with the `gmp` feature, against those of `src/mpfr_calls/mpfr.rs`, which call MPFR. Those in `src/dyadic/log/tests/mpfr_cases.rs` take the arguments of MPFR's own tests of these functions, with MPFR's results at 53 bits where those tests give them, and those in `src/dyadic/log/tests/levels.rs` force each level from 1 to 64 limbs at a few arguments, as for the exponentials above.

`atan_dir_correct` in [`DyAtan.v`](theories/DyAtan.v) shows that `atan(x, up)` of `src/dyadic/atan.rs`, which computes atan x with the kernel, returns, at a finite `x` and where it returns rather than panics, atan x rounded toward +∞ if `up` and toward -∞ otherwise, as `mpfr_round` says. `atan2_dir_correct` in `DyAtan.v`, and `asin_dir_correct` and `acos_dir_correct` in [`DyAsin.v`](theories/DyAsin.v), state the same of `atan2`, `asin` and `acos`, with `Ratan2`, `asin` and `acos` in place of `atan`, at finite `y` and `x` with `y <> 0` or `x > 0` for `atan2(y, x, up)`, and at a finite `x` in `asin_dom` and in `acos_dom`, which are `[-1, 1]`. `DyadicAtan.atan_dir x up` in `Model.v` is `atan(x, up)`, with `None` where the Rust panics. Each function first settles some arguments by rules: NaN, the infinities, the arguments outside the domains of `asin` and `acos`, the zeros and infinities of `atan2`, where it returns the values that the tests check against `mpfr_atan2`, those of magnitude at least 2^54 for `atan`, whose results round as ±π/2 does, those of magnitude below 2^-26 for `atan` and 2^-27 for `asin`, whose results are within an ulp of `x`, and, for `atan2` with `x > 0`, the quotients `y / x` that are `f64`s of magnitude below 2^-26, whose results are within an ulp of `y / x`. At the finite arguments in the domains, the theorems above meet the rules `atan_huge_rule` in `DyAtan.v` and `atan_tiny_rule` and `asin_tiny_rule` in [`DyAtanTiny.v`](theories/DyAtanTiny.v), with `tiny_quotient_correct` there for the quotients of `atan2`, and the values of `atan2` at `y = ±0`, which is `y` for `x > 0`, and at `x = ±0`, which `pi_times` rounds as ±π/2 (`pi_times_correct`). Each of these lemmas shows that its rule returns the rounding in its direction. `atan_dir_infinity`, `atan2_dir_inf_pos`, `atan2_dir_inf_neg_pos`, `atan2_dir_inf_neg_neg` and `atan2_dir_y_inf` give the results at the infinities that `Contract.v` asks for. Ziv's strategy settles 1 for `acos`, whose result 0 is an `f64`, because its evaluation gives the exact enclosure [0, 0] there, as the tests check. The arguments that no rule settles go to `ziv_correct`, with the enclosures that `ev_atan_correct`, `ev_atan2_correct`, `ev_asin_correct` and `ev_acos_correct` show hold the result at each level. These build on [`DyAtanCore.v`](theories/DyAtanCore.v). `DyAtanCore.series_correct` shows that `series`, where it returns, encloses atan of every point of its argument, by Horner's scheme on the scaled remainders of the series of atan (`atan_tail_bounds` and `atan_horner` in [`AtanSeries.v`](theories/AtanSeries.v)), from an enclosure of `[0, 1/(2k+1)]` for the remainder after the `k = terms n z` terms that it sums, at most 898 (`ac_terms`). `atan_series_correct` shows the same of `atan_series`, which takes `series` at its argument `u` if the upper bound of `u` is at most 2^-8, and otherwise twice `series` at `u / (1 + √(1 + u^2))` (`atan_halve`). `atan_encl_correct` shows that `atan_encl`, where it returns, encloses atan of every point of its argument `w`, whose lower bound its assertion keeps at least 0, as `π/4 - atan((1 - w)/(1 + w))`, `π/4 + atan((w - 1)/(w + 1))` or `π/2 - atan(1/w)` where it does not take the series at `w` (`atan_quarter_minus`, `atan_quarter_plus` and `atan_half_inv` in [`AtanIdent.v`](theories/AtanIdent.v)), and `asin_parts_correct` that `asin_parts`, where it returns, encloses asin t or acos t for `0 <= t <= 1`, as atan of `t / √((1 - t)(1 + t))` or of its inverse (`asin_as_atan` and `acos_as_atan`). `pi_correct` in [`DyConstPi.v`](theories/DyConstPi.v) shows that `pi` encloses π. The table of π in `src/dyadic/consts.rs` is `floor(π * 2^4222)`, its first 4,224 bits, and `DyConstPi.v` shows it with `vm_compute` of sums of the series of atan in Machin's formula `π = 16 atan(1/5) - 4 atan(1/239)` (Stdlib's `Machin_4_5_239`) at 4,288 bits, each term floored, whose remainders `atan_tail_bounds` bounds:

```coq
Theorem atan2_dir_correct :
  forall y x up v,
  is_finite y = true -> is_finite x = true -> (B2R y <> 0 \/ 0 < B2R x)%R ->
  DyadicAtan.atan2_dir y x up = Some v ->
  mpfr_round f64_format (if up then RNDU else RNDD) (Ratan2 (B2R y) (B2R x)) (with_val v).

Lemma atan_encl_correct :
  forall n w c E wv,
  level_ok n -> encl_ok n w -> encl_le E w -> 0 <= E <= 2 ^ 30 -> encl_has w wv ->
  DyadicAtan.atan_encl n w = Some c ->
  encl_ok n c /\ encl_le (2 ^ 51) c /\ encl_has c (atan wv).

Lemma pi_correct :
  forall n p,
  level_ok n -> DyadicConsts.pi n = Some p ->
  encl_ok n p /\ encl_le (2 ^ 15) p /\ encl_has p PI.
```

`k_atan_c`, `k_asin_c`, `k_acos_c` and `k_atan2_c` in `DyPoint.v` show that `k_atan`, `k_asin`, `k_acos` and `k_atan2`, the kernel's four functions as fields of a record `point`, satisfy `atan_c`, `asin_c`, `acos_c` and `atan2_c` of `Contract.v`, with `kernel_field_correct_dom` for `asin` and `acos`, and `kernel_field2_correct`, its counterpart for a function of two `f64`s, for `atan2`, so that `kernel_atan_correct` to `kernel_atan2_correct` give `atan_correct`, `asin_correct`, `acos_correct` and `atan2_correct` for a record `point` whose fields are these:

```coq
Theorem k_atan2_c : atan2_c k_atan2.

Theorem kernel_atan2_correct :
  forall p y x, valid y -> valid x ->
  exists z, Interval.atan2 (kernel_point p) y x = Some z /\
    is_tightest (image2_set atan2_dom Ratan2 y x) z.
```

The tests in `src/dyadic/atan/tests.rs` take these four functions, in both directions, at the special values, at each rule's threshold and the arguments next to it, at the edges of the cases of `atan_encl` and `asin_parts`, where `atan_series` starts to halve its argument, at arguments whose results are close to an `f64`, at the hardest arguments that searches found, and at random arguments, of any exponent, uniform in intervals and log-uniform over the exponents, for `atan2` in all four quadrants and with `|y / x|` from about 2^-2097 to 2^2097. They check that the two directions give the same `f64` or two adjacent ones, or NaN both, and, with the `gmp` feature, that they give those of `src/mpfr_calls/mpfr.rs`, which call MPFR, bit for bit, and that the table of π is `floor(π * 2^4222)`, from π rounded down and up by MPFR at 4,300 bits. Those in `src/dyadic/atan/tests/mpfr_cases.rs` take the arguments of MPFR's own tests of these functions, with MPFR's results at 53 bits where those tests give them, and those in `src/dyadic/atan/tests/levels.rs` force each level from 1 to 64 limbs at a few arguments, and for π itself, as for the exponentials above.

`sin_dir_correct` in [`DyTrig.v`](theories/DyTrig.v) shows that `sin(x, up)` of `src/dyadic/trig.rs`, which computes sin x with the kernel, returns, at a finite `x` and where it returns rather than panics, sin x rounded toward +∞ if `up` and toward -∞ otherwise, as `mpfr_round` says. `cos_dir_correct` and `tan_dir_correct` state the same of `cos` and `tan`, at every finite `x`: `tan_dir_correct` needs no hypothesis on the domain of tan, as `ev_tan` returns only where the divisor of its quotient excludes 0. `DyadicTrig.sin_dir x up` in `Model.v` is `sin(x, up)`, with `None` where the Rust panics. Each function first settles some arguments by rules: NaN and the infinities, at which it returns NaN and of which `Contract.v` asks nothing, and those of magnitude below 2^-26, whose results are within an ulp of `x` for sin and tan and of 1 for cos. At the finite arguments, the theorems above meet the rules `sin_tiny_rule`, `cos_tiny_rule` and `tan_tiny_rule` in [`DyTrigTiny.v`](theories/DyTrigTiny.v), each of which shows that its rule returns the rounding in its direction. The arguments that no rule settles go to `ziv_correct`, with the enclosures that `ev_sin_correct`, `ev_cos_correct` and `ev_tan_correct` show hold the result at each level. Each evaluation takes `t = |x|`, as sin and tan are odd and cos is even, and reduces it: `reduce_correct` in [`DyTrigReduce.v`](theories/DyTrigReduce.v) shows that `t = k π/2 + ρ`, or `t = k π/2 - ρ`, for an integer `k` whose `k mod 4` is the quadrant that `reduce` returns and a `ρ` in the enclosure that it returns, with `|ρ| <= 1` from its `assert!`. `reduce` divides `t 2^(s+1)`, an integer, by the top `l` limbs `P` of the table of π (`div_rem_correct` of [`Knuth.v`](theories/Knuth.v)), and `PI_top` bounds `t - k π/2` for whatever integer `k` the division gives, so that `k` is the nearest integer to `t / (π/2)` is not needed and not proved. `DyTrigCore.series_correct` in [`DyTrigCore.v`](theories/DyTrigCore.v) shows that `series`, where it returns, encloses sin or cos of every point of its argument in `[-1, 1]`, by Horner's scheme on the scaled remainders of the series of sin and cos (`trig_tail_bounds` and `trig_horner` in [`TrigSeries.v`](theories/TrigSeries.v)), from the enclosure `[0, 1]` of the remainder after the `terms n z` terms that it sums, at most 389 (`tc_terms`), and `sin_k_correct` that `sin_k` encloses `sin(k π/2 + ρ)` or `sin(k π/2 - ρ)` for every `k` with the `k mod 4` that it takes (`sin_quad`). `ev_cos` takes cos t as sin(t + π/2), with the quadrant plus 1, and `ev_tan` divides sin ρ by cos ρ for an even `k` and cos ρ by sin ρ for an odd one (`tan_quad`), with cos ρ as `√((1 - sin ρ)(1 + sin ρ))` (`cos_sqrt`):

```coq
Theorem sin_dir_correct :
  forall x up v,
  is_finite x = true -> DyadicTrig.sin_dir x up = Some v ->
  mpfr_round f64_format (if up then RNDU else RNDD) (sin (B2R x)) (with_val v).

Lemma reduce_correct :
  forall n t quad neg r,
  level_ok n -> is_finite t = true -> (0 <= B2R t)%R ->
  DyadicTrig.reduce n t = Some (quad, neg, r) ->
  encl_ok n r /\ encl_le (2 ^ 15) r /\
  exists k rho, k mod 4 = quad /\ encl_has r rho /\ (-1 <= rho <= 1)%R /\
    B2R t = (IZR k * (PI / 2) + (if neg then - rho else rho))%R.
```

`k_sin_c`, `k_cos_c` and `k_tan_c` in `DyPoint.v` show that `k_sin`, `k_cos` and `k_tan`, the kernel's three functions as fields of a record `point`, satisfy `sin_c`, `cos_c` and `tan_c` of `Contract.v`, with `kernel_field_correct_dom` for `tan`, whose domain the proof does not use, so that `kernel_sin_correct` to `kernel_tan_correct` give `sin_correct`, `cos_correct` and `tan_correct` for a record `point` whose fields are these. Each of them uses both `k_sin_c` and `k_cos_c`, as `Interval::sin`, `Interval::cos` and `Interval::tan` call both functions through `quadrant`, and `kernel_tan_correct` also `k_tan_c`:

```coq
Theorem k_sin_c : sin_c k_sin.

Theorem kernel_sin_correct :
  forall p has_fma first x, valid x ->
  exists y, Interval.sin (kernel_point p) has_fma first x = Some y /\
    is_tightest (image_dom real_dom sin x) y.
```

The tests in `src/dyadic/trig/tests.rs` take these three functions, in both directions, at the special values, at each rule's threshold and the arguments next to it, at `NO_REDUCE` and π/4, between which `reduce` takes `k = 0`, at arguments whose results are close to an `f64`, at 6381956970095103 × 2^797, the case of Kahan and McDonald, which is close to a multiple of π/2, and the arguments next to it, at the hardest arguments that four searches found, and at random arguments, of any exponent, uniform in [-10, 10) and log-uniform from 2^60 up, and, with the `gmp` feature, at the `f64`s nearest to multiples of π/2 at every exponent from 0 to 1023 and the arguments next to them. They check that the two directions give the same `f64` or two adjacent ones, or NaN both, and, with the `gmp` feature, that they give those of `src/mpfr_calls/mpfr.rs`, which call MPFR, bit for bit. Those in `src/dyadic/trig/tests/mpfr_cases.rs` take the arguments of MPFR's own tests of these functions, with MPFR's results at 53 bits where those tests give them, and those in `src/dyadic/trig/tests/levels.rs` force each level from 1 to 64 limbs at a few arguments, near multiples of π/2 and huge ones among them, as for the exponentials above.

`E_tightest` in [`ConstLn.v`](theories/ConstLn.v) shows that `Interval::E` and `DecInterval::E` are the tightest intervals around e, the base of natural logarithms, as the doc comment of `Interval::E` says, and that of `DecInterval::E`, which is `Interval::E` decorated `com`, and that this decoration is the strongest (`strongest0`):

```coq
Theorem E_tightest :
  is_tightest (fun r => r = exp 1) Interval.E /\
  is_tightest (fun r => r = exp 1) (itv DecInterval.E) /\
  strongest0 (dec DecInterval.E) (exp 1) (itv DecInterval.E).
```
