- `F64X2` is a pair, and so are the array `[Self; 2]` that `mul_rev_to_pair` returns and the array `[f64; 2]` that `extract` returns. `lanewise2` takes the two paths as functions. `sqrt1_slow` takes `f` as a function too, one that returns an `option` like the models passed for it.
- `sqrt1_rd_impl` and `sqrt1_ru_impl` call themselves through `sqrt1_slow`. Rocq functions must terminate, so the models take the number of nested calls they may make and return `None` if they would make more. `sqrt1_rd` and `sqrt1_ru` allow one. A result `Some r` means that the Rust function returns `r`.
- A const generic parameter, such as `FMA`, is an ordinary argument. So is `HAS_FMA`, which `mul_ru`, `div_ru`, `mul_add_ru`, `sqrt1_rd` and `sqrt1_ru` pass for it, as its value depends on the target. `Interval::mul` and `Interval::sqr`, which call `mul_ru`, `Interval::mul_add`, which calls `mul_add_ru`, `Interval::sqrt`, which calls `sqrt1_ru` and `sqrt_rd_ru`, `Interval::recip`, `Interval::div` and its ten helpers, and `Interval::mul_rev_to_pair`, which call `div_ru`, `Interval::cos`, `Interval::sin` and `Interval::tan`, which call `Interval::div`, and `tan` also `Interval::mul`, and their decorated versions take it as the argument `has_fma`.
- `f64::floor`, `f64::ceil`, `f64::round` and `f64::trunc` are Flocq's `Bnearbyint` toward -∞, toward +∞, to nearest with ties away from zero, and toward zero, IEEE 754's roundToIntegralTowardNegative, roundToIntegralTowardPositive, roundToIntegralTiesToAway and roundToIntegralTowardZero. `f64::abs` is Flocq's `Babs`. `f64::is_finite` in `Interval::contains` is Flocq's `is_finite`, which is false exactly for the infinities and NaN, as `f64::is_finite` is, and its `&` of two `bool`s is `&&`, which gives the same value. `x.copysign(y)` is `x` if `x` and `y` have the same sign, and `-x` otherwise. As NaNs have no sign in the model, it takes a NaN `y` as positive, but its only callers, `round_ties_even1` and the functions of `DyadicAtan`, never pass a NaN for `y`. `p.is_sign_negative()` in `mul1_ru_tiny` is Flocq's `Bsign p`, which differs from it only for a NaN, and `p = x * y` is not NaN there, as `x` and `y` are finite. `wx.partial_cmp(&wy)` in `Interval::cancel_minus` is Flocq's `Bcompare`, which, like `f64::partial_cmp`, returns `None` if either is NaN, and otherwise compares the values, with `0.0` equal to `-0.0` and -∞ and +∞ below and above every other float. `Less`, `Equal` and `Greater` are `Lt`, `Eq` and `Gt`, and `unreachable!()` is `None`.
- `Interval` is a record of its `rep`, and `DecInterval` of an `Interval` and a `Decoration`, whose variants compare by their discriminants, as the derived `Ord` does. `valid` states the form of `rep` from the comment on `struct Interval`, with the bounds that `TryFrom<(f64, f64)>` accepts. The theorems assume that their argument is `valid`, which `made_valid` in `Valid.v` proves for the intervals that the constructors make and the proved operations compute from them.
- A comparison of two `F64X2`s, such as `lt`, returns a mask, whose lanes have every bit set where the comparison holds and every bit clear where it does not. A lane with every bit set is a NaN, which `to_bits` would give the bits of another NaN, so the model keeps the bit patterns of the lanes of a mask instead. `all` and `bitmask` take these bit patterns, and so does `and` as its second argument. `and` reads the lanes of its first argument with `to_bits`, and `or` those of both of its arguments, which gives their bits only if they are not NaN. `Interval::sign`, their only caller, passes no NaN: it calls `and` on `1.0` and `-1.0`, and `or` on the results, whose lanes are `1.0`, `-1.0` or `0.0`.
- `max1` and `min1` read `to_bits` only of two zeros, whose bit patterns the model gives exactly. `f64::NAN` in `maximum1` and `minimum1` is the model's only NaN.
- `unsafe { transmute(c) }` in `classify`, from a `u8` to an `IntervalClass`, is `transmute_class`, which returns `None` unless `c` is the discriminant of a variant, as the behaviour is undefined then. The variants `E`, `M`, `N0`, `N1`, `P0`, `P1` and `Z` are `CE`, `CM` and so on, as `Z` is the type of integers. The casts `bitmask(...) as u8` keep the value, which is below 4, and are left out, and `|` of two `bool`s in `either_empty` is `||`, which gives the same value. `transmute` in `classify2`, from a `u8` to an `IntervalClass2`, is `transmute_class2`, which returns `None` unless `c / 16` and `c % 16` are both discriminants of `IntervalClass`, and otherwise the pair of the variants that `transmute_class` decodes them to, written `(CM, CN0)` for the variant `M_N0` and so on. That is the variant with discriminant `c`, as every discriminant of `IntervalClass` is below 16. The casts `c as u8` of an `IntervalClass` in `classify2` are `class_discriminant`.
- `HAS_MAXIMUM` depends on the backend: it is `true` in `generic.rs` and `false` in `x86_64.rs`. So `Interval::max`, `Interval::min` and their decorated versions take it as the argument `has_maximum`, as for `HAS_FMA`, and the theorems hold for either value. The branch for `false` calls `either_empty` and the backend's `max` and `min`, which are the portable backend's here, as every backend function in the model is.
- The places that make a `DecInterval`, for `ComCommon.v`, are the ones listed above. `git grep -n 'new_unchecked\|transmute' -- src` finds the callers of `new_unchecked` and the `transmute`s, and `git grep -nE '(Self|DecInterval) *\{|\b[xd]: ' -- src/interval/dec_interval.rs` finds the declarations of the fields `x` and `d`, without `pub`, and the one struct expression of `DecInterval`, in `new_unchecked`. [`check-sites.sh`](check-sites.sh), which CI runs on every push and pull request to `main`, fails if the lines that these two greps find differ from those in [`sites.txt`](sites.txt). It compares the lines without their line numbers and indentation, so it fails if a line is added, removed, changed or moved to another file, but not if it only moves within its file. In particular, it fails if the line of a field declaration changes, for example to `pub(crate) x: Interval,`. It also fails if this file no longer gives one of the six greps of this bullet and the next one verbatim. That the two greps find every place that makes a `DecInterval`, and that the places among their lines are the ones listed above, is still checked by reading, and so is that `src/interval/dec_interval.rs` declares no module, has no struct expression other than the one in `new_unchecked`, neither assigns to a field nor takes a mutable reference to one, and derives only `Clone`, `Copy` and `Debug`, which copy or read the fields, and that the other `unsafe` code outside `src/simd` only calls MPFR. The greps find the body of `def_com!` but not its uses, so the compiler checks the bounds of each use instead. `def_com!` reads the lanes `[-a; b]` of the constant of `Interval` that it decorates `com` with a `transmute` into `[f64; 2]`, the reverse of the one in `const_interval!`, and asserts in a `const` that -∞ < `a` <= `b` < +∞ (`def_com_ok` in `Model.v`), so that a use of the macro compiles only if this holds, whatever made that constant. `def_com_ok_bounds` shows that the constant of `Interval` is then `const_interval a b` for finite floats `a` and `b` with `const_interval_ok a b`, as `def_com_com_common` and `dmade_def_com` in `Valid.v` assume. What is left to read is that `def_com_ok` transcribes the assertion, with the two lanes that the `transmute` gives as the pair `rep`. `try_from_be_bytes` and its siblings take the floats `a` and `b` that `f64::from_be_bytes` and so on decode, which the model leaves out, as the theorem holds for any. Their `a.is_sign_negative()` is Flocq's `Bsign a` and `b.is_sign_positive()` its negation, which differ from Rust only for a NaN, where `a <= b` already makes the condition false. The `transmute` in `const_dec_interval!` puts an `Interval` and a `Decoration` into the fields of a `DecInterval`, as both structs are `#[repr(C)]` with the same fields in the same order, which is checked by reading too. `f64::NEG_INFINITY` and `f64::INFINITY` are `B754_infinity true` and `B754_infinity false`.
- The places that make an `Interval`, for `Valid.v`, are the ones listed there and the operations. [`check-sites.sh`](check-sites.sh) also runs the four greps below, in CI as above. It fails if one of the two that must find nothing finds something, or if the lines that the other two find differ from those in [`sites.txt`](sites.txt), compared as for `ComCommon.v`. The rest of this argument is still checked by reading. The field `rep` of `Interval` is `pub(crate)`, so only the library can make an `Interval` with a struct expression. `git grep -nE '\brep *:' -- src ':!src/simd'` finds every struct expression of `Interval`, as none uses the shorthand `{ rep }` (`git grep -nE '\{ *rep *[,}]' -- src` finds nothing), and `git grep -n 'with_infsup_raw\|transmute' -- src ':!src/simd'` finds the calls of `with_infsup_raw` and the `transmute`s, of which those in `Interval::EMPTY` and `const_interval!` make an `Interval`. As for `ComCommon.v`, the other `unsafe` code outside `src/simd` only calls MPFR, and the backends in `src/simd` make no `Interval`. Nothing assigns to `rep` (`git grep -nE '\.rep *=[^=]' -- src ':!src/simd'` finds nothing), and `x += y` and the other compound assignments assign what an operation returns. Each struct expression and each call of `with_infsup_raw` is in one of those places, in a proved operation, or in `floors_exact`, which only `cos`, `sin` and `tan` call, and whose singletons `[a, a]` and `[b, b]` of finite floats are `valid` (`tc_with_valid` in `TrigCert.v`) and are not returned. The places that make a `DecInterval` are those of `ComCommon.v`, above, and `DecInterval::interval` returns the interval of one. A `Result` is an `option`, with `Ok(x)` as `Some x` and the `Err` as `None`. `const_interval_ok` is the condition that `const_interval!` asserts in a `const`, so that a use of the macro compiles only if it is true. `def_com!` asserts `def_com_ok` in the same way, and `def_com_ok_bounds` shows that this gives `const_interval_ok` for the bounds of the constant that it decorates, as `dmade_def_com` assumes. `From<DecNInterval>` and `try_from_ninterval_exact` take what the grammar of `src/parse.rs` returns, and the theorems of `Valid.v` about them hold for any `DecNInterval` and `NInterval`, with no hypothesis about the grammar, for a call that does not panic.
- The conversions of `src/parse.rs` are modelled from the `NInterval` or `DecNInterval` that the grammar returns. The grammar, apart from the step that decorates a literal, is not modelled (see "What is not proved yet"), and `ninterval_ok` in `Model.v` states what it is checked by reading to return: `infsup_interval` returns `InfSup(a, b)` only if `a <= b`, `a` is not `Infinity` and `b` is not `NegInfinity`, and the other rules return `NInterval::EMPTY`, `NInterval::ENTIRE`, a `Point` of a rational, or, in `uncertain_interval`, the bounds `center - radius` or `center`, and `center + radius` or `center`, for a radius that is not negative, with -∞ and +∞ in place of `center - radius` and `center + radius` for an unbounded radius. And each bound that is a `Rational` has `rat_ok`, as `number_ok` states: its denominator is positive, as the comment of `Rat` says, its exponents `e2` and `e5` are `i32`s, and its numerator and denominator have at most `2^63` bits, `2^60` bytes, more memory than any machine has. `Model.v` transcribes the step of the grammar that decorates a literal: `NInterval::EMPTY`, `DecNInterval::EMPTY`, `DecNInterval::NAI`, `DecNInterval::new` and `DecNInterval::set_dec` as `n_EMPTY`, `dn_EMPTY`, `dn_NAI`, `dn_new` and `dn_set_dec`, and the arm `(Ok(x), Some(d))` of the closure in `decorated_interval`, which returns `DecNInterval::set_dec(x, d)` if its decoration is `d` and rejects the literal otherwise, as `dn_decorated`, with `Ok(xd)` as `Some xd` and the `Err` as `None`. `ParseDec.v` proves `dec_ninterval_ok` of `dn_NAI`, and of what `dn_new`, `dn_set_dec` and `dn_decorated` return for an `x` with `ninterval_ok` (see `from_dec_ninterval_correct` above). Besides `ninterval_ok`, two things are checked by reading. The `nom` combinators of `decorated_interval` call this step: `alt` returns `Ok(DecNInterval::NAI)` for `[nai]`, and otherwise `map` applies the closure to what `interval` and `opt(preceded(char('_'), decoration))` return, so that the arm `(Ok(x), None)` returns `DecNInterval::new(x)` for a literal without a decoration and the arm `(Ok(x), Some(d))` is `dn_decorated x d` for one with a decoration `d`. And `FromStr` for `DecInterval` passes the `x` of `Ok(("", Ok(x)))` to `From<DecNInterval>`. A `Rational` holds a `Rat` of `src/parse/rat.rs`, the record `Rat` of `Model.v`, with each `Nat` as the `Z` that it holds, whose limbs are `NatLimbs.to_limbs` of it, and the value `rat_R`, `(-1)^neg * num * 2^e2 * 5^e5 / den`, which need not be in lowest terms. That reading of the limbs needs a `Nat` to have no zero limb at the top, as the comment of `src/nat.rs` says. The operations of `Nat` that `Model.v` transcribes keep that (`NatOps.v`), and that `Nat::from_dec`, `Nat::from_hex` and `-`, with which the grammar computes the `Nat`s of a `Rat`, return no zero limb at the top is checked by reading. `NInterval::InfSup` and `NInterval::Point` are `NInfSup` and `NPoint`, the derived `PartialOrd` of `Number` is `number_le`, which compares the variants in the order of their declaration and two `Rat`s by `impl Ord for Rat`, taken to compare their values (see "What is not proved yet"), and `x == NInterval::EMPTY` is `nis_empty x`. `Model.v` transcribes `scale`, `round_up_scaled`, `rational_to_f64` and `number_to_f64`, which round a bound to an `f64` with no call to MPFR, as functions that return an `option`, with `None` where an `i128` operation would overflow or an `as` cast to `u64` or `i32` would change the value (`checked_i` and `checked_u`), or where a `Nat` would be divided by zero, which panics (`NatLimbs.div_rem_z`). The theorems show that they return `Some` for a bound with `number_ok`. `round_up_scaled` calls `Generic.round_up_exact`, the model of `round_up_exact` in `src/simd/generic.rs`.
- `Model.v` transcribes the functions of `src/format/digits.rs` as `format_decompose`, `format_exact_dec`, `format_away`, `format_cut_dec`, `format_fixed`, `format_sci`, `format_cut_bin` and `format_hex`, which return an `option`, with `None` where an `i64`, `u64` or `u32` operation would overflow, a `u64` would be shifted by 64 or more, or a `Nat` would be divided by zero (`checked_i`, `checked_u`, `shl` and `shr`, and `NatLimbs.div_rem_z`). As for `src/parse.rs`, each `Nat` is the `Z` that it holds, with the limbs `NatLimbs.to_limbs` of it, and `Nat::from_u64`, `Nat::pow5`, `<<`, `*`, `+`, `div_rem`, `is_zero` and `digits10` are their loops of `NatLimbs` on those limbs, which `NatOps.v` proves to compute the operations on `Z`, `digits10` Flocq's `Zdigits radix10`. The casts that could change a value are written as Rust computes them: `as i64` of a `u64` or a `usize` as `(z + 2^63) mod 2^64 - 2^63`, `as u64` as `z mod 2^64` and `as u32` as `z mod 2^32`, while `i64::from` and `u64::from` keep the value. A precision is a `usize`, an integer `p` with `0 <= p < 2^64` on the 64-bit targets on which inari supports the feature `gmp`, which `src/format.rs` needs, and on smaller ones too. The functions cast it only after capping it at 1100, or in `hex` below 13, so that the casts keep its value whatever the width of `usize`. `!(1 << 63)` is `2 ^ 63 - 1`, and `x.is_sign_negative()` is `Bsign x`, as `x` is not NaN there. The rest of `src/format.rs` is not modelled.
- `Limbs`, `NatLimbs` and `Dyadic` in `Model.v` transcribe the loops of `src/limbs.rs`, the operations of `Nat` above and the dyadic kernel of `src/dyadic.rs` with each slice of limbs as a `list Z`, the least significant limb first. A function that writes a slice `out: &mut [u64]` returns its new contents. The bounds checks of slices, `split_at_mut` and the `usize` arithmetic on lengths and bit counts are not modelled: for the lengths that the callers pass, every range and index is in bounds and none of that arithmetic overflows, which is checked by reading. Nor are `with_buf`, which gives the kernel its scratch limbs, the scratch of `limbs::div_rem`, and the buffer that `reduce` of `src/dyadic/trig.rs` splits into its slices and that scratch: each function writes every limb of the scratch that it reads before it reads it, so its contents do not matter. Products of limbs in `u128` are checked (`checked_u 128`), `overflowing_add` and `overflowing_sub` are `ovf_add` and `ovf_sub`, and `expect`, `assert!` and `panic!` are `None`. The loop of D3 in `knuth_step`, the `loop` of `isqrt` and the `while` of `digits10` take fuel, which the theorems show is enough. The const generic `N` of `Dyadic<N>` is the `nat` `n`, and the evaluation `f.eval::<N>()` that Ziv's strategy calls is a function `ev` of `n`. `isqrt` seeds Newton's iteration with bits of `(t[0] as f64).sqrt()`, which the model computes with `of_u64` and `fsqrt`, but the proof uses only that the 32 bits it keeps, with the implicit bit set, are at least 2^31 (`seed_bounds`), which holds for every `f64`, so it holds whatever `f64::sqrt` returns.
- `DyadicConsts`, `DyadicExp`, `DyadicLog`, `DyadicAtan` and `DyadicTrig` in `Model.v` transcribe `src/dyadic/consts.rs`, `src/dyadic/exp.rs`, `src/dyadic/log.rs`, `src/dyadic/atan.rs` and `src/dyadic/trig.rs` in the same way, the tables of ln 2, ln 10 and π as lists of the limbs of the Rust arrays, in the same order. `c as i64` of an `f64` is `to_i64`, which rounds toward zero and saturates, and gives 0 for NaN, as Rust's cast does, and `as usize` of an integer from 1 to 22 keeps its value. `x.ceil()` and `x.floor()` are `fceil` and `ffloor`, as above, and `<`, `>`, `<=` and `>=` of `Dyadic`s are those of `PartialOrd`, which compares with `cmp`. The literals `1e1` to `1e22` of `POW10` are `binary_normalize` of their values rounded to nearest, as for `kept as f64` above, and the other literals, `LOG2_E` and `LOG2_10` of `std::f64::consts` among them, are given by their bit patterns. In `DyadicLog`, `f64::NAN` is the model's NaN, `POW10.iter().position(|&p| p == x)` is the first `k` from 0 to 22 with `eq (pow10 k) x`, `k as f64` is `of_u64 k`, the `i128` sum of `reduce`, which cannot overflow, is computed without a check, and `x.is_infinite()` is `x == f64::INFINITY || x == f64::NEG_INFINITY`. In `DyadicAtan`, `x.abs()` is `fabs x`, `y.is_infinite()` is as in `DyadicLog`, `x.is_sign_positive()` is `negb (Bsign x)`, as `x` is not NaN there, the `Option<f64>` that `tiny_quotient` returns is an `option f64` inside the `option` of the model, and `a.exp - b.exp` in `tiny_quotient` and the arithmetic of `terms` on `u64`s and `i64`s, which cannot overflow, are computed without a check. In `DyadicTrig`, `f64::NAN` is the model's NaN, `x.abs()` is `fabs x`, `-t` is `opp t`, `&PI[66 - l..]` is `skipn`, `c.copy_from_slice(p)` and the `sub_assign` after it in `reduce` are `c`, whose borrow is dropped, as is the carry of its `add_assign`, `(d.exp + 64) as usize` there is `Z.to_nat`, as `d.exp + 64` is not negative, `u64::from(odd)` is `Z.b2z odd`, `a != b` of two `bool`s is `xorb`, and the arithmetic of `reduce`, `terms`, `series` and the `eval` of `Cos` on `i64`s and `u64`s, which cannot overflow, is computed without a check. The functions return an `option`, with `None` where the Rust panics: at an `assert!` of `src/dyadic/exp.rs`, `src/dyadic/log.rs`, `src/dyadic/atan.rs` or `src/dyadic/trig.rs`, at the cap of `ziv`, or at a panic of an operation of the kernel. The fields of a record `point` are total functions, so `kernel_field` in `DyPoint.v` takes the kernel's result where the model returns one, and the result rounded by definition, `round_f64` of `MpfrIdeal.v`, where the Rust panics. The theorems about `kernel_point` therefore cover the calls that return, and say nothing about the calls that panic. That the loop ends before its cap is not proved (see "What is not proved yet").
- MPFR is modelled only for the functions of `src/elementary.rs` that are proved here, and only as far as `mpfr_fn!`, `mpfr_fn2!` and `mpfr_fn_si!` in `src/mpfr_calls/mpfr.rs` call it. An MPFR number is an `mpfr_t`: NaN, an infinity or a zero with its sign, or a real other than 0. `Float::with_val(f64::MANTISSA_DIGITS, x)` is `with_val x`, an MPFR number of 53 bits that holds `x` exactly, keeping the sign of a zero and of an infinity, which needs an exponent range that contains that of `f64` (`range_ok` in `Mpfr.v`). The MPFR functions that these macros call and `mpfr::get_d` are the fields of a record `m : mpfr`, each a function of the rounding direction and of an MPFR number, of two for `mpfr_atan2` and `mpfr_pow`, and of an MPFR number and an integer for `mpfr_pow_si`, which `Model.v` leaves abstract. Every model of an elementary function takes as its first argument a record `pt : point` with a field `p_f` for each pair of functions `f_rd` and `f_ru` that these macros define, with `f_rd pt` as `p_f pt RNDD` and `f_ru pt` as `p_f pt RNDU`. The theorems pass it `mpfr_point m`, which `Model.v` makes of `m`: its field `p_exp` is `mpfr_fn m (mpfr_exp m)`, and the others are made the same way of the MPFR function that the macro calls, with `mpfr_fn2` for `mpfr_fn2!` and `mpfr_fn_si` for `mpfr_fn_si!`, and a coercion lets the theorems write it as `m`. `mpfr::f(x.as_raw_mut(), x.as_raw(), rnd)` sets `x` to `f rnd x`, as MPFR lets the result be the argument (§4.3 of the MPFR manual), and its ternary result, which the code ignores, is left out. In the same way, in `mpfr_fn2!`, `mpfr::atan2(x.as_raw_mut(), x.as_raw(), y.as_raw(), rnd)` sets `x` to `mpfr_atan2 rnd x y`, and `mpfr::pow(x.as_raw_mut(), x.as_raw(), y.as_raw(), rnd)` to `mpfr_pow rnd x y`, where `y` is `with_val y` too, and in `mpfr_fn_si!`, `mpfr::pow_si(x.as_raw_mut(), x.as_raw(), y.into(), rnd)` sets `x` to `mpfr_pow_si rnd x n`, where `n` is the integer that the `i32` `y` holds, which `y.into()` converts to a `c_long` exactly. The `let` arm of each of these macros first calls `assert_thread_safe` in `src/mpfr_calls/mpfr.rs`, which is left out. `mpfr::rnd_t::RNDD` and `RNDU` are `RNDD` and `RNDU`. `(-a).max(b)` in `Interval::cosh` is `fmax first (opp a) b`: the larger of the two, or, if they compare equal, `-a` if `first` and `b` otherwise, as Rust's `f64::max` may return either. Neither is NaN there. `x.max(y)` and `x.min(y)` in `Interval::cos` and `Interval::sin`, of two results of `mpfr_fn!` functions at finite floats, which are not NaN, are `fmax first x y` and `fmin first x y` in the same way, and each path calls at most one of them. `z_ad.min(z_bc)` and `z_ac.max(z_bd)` in `Interval::pow`, two calls on the same path, are `fmin first_min` and `fmax first_max`, with an argument for each. Their arguments are results of `mpfr_fn2!` functions, which are not NaN there, as their lanes show (`pxl_not_nan` and `pxh_not_nan`). `Interval::cos`, `Interval::sin` and `Interval::tan_impl` call `floors_exact` only if `n == 0.0` and the test of `b - a` before it are false, and `floors_exact` divides `[b, b]` only if its first test is true, which the model writes with `if`s, as the divisions return an `option`. The `match` in `quadrant` on two `bool`s is nested `if`s. `||` in `impl_log!` and `Interval::atanh` and in `interior`, and `|` in `subset`, are Rocq's `||`, which gives the same value.

[`Mpfr.v`](theories/Mpfr.v) states what the theorems about the functions of `src/elementary.rs` assume of MPFR, and has to be checked by reading against the MPFR manual (of MPFR 4.2.2, which `gmp-mpfr-sys` 1.7.1 bundles), as `Model.v` is against the Rust code. `mpfr_ok_consistent` in `MpfrConsistent.v` shows that the hypotheses can all hold at once, for an ideal MPFR (see "What is proved"), but not that the MPFR that the Rust code links satisfies them. The unit tests in [`src/elementary/mpfr_tests.rs`](../src/elementary/mpfr_tests.rs), which need the feature `gmp`, call the linked MPFR through the code's own calls (the arms of `mpfr_fn!`, `mpfr_fn2!` and `mpfr_fn_si!` that give the MPFR number before `get_d`, and `get_d` itself, in `src/mpfr_calls/mpfr.rs`) on every special value that the hypotheses state, in each direction they state it for, at a few arguments where a clause holds for many, on `mpfr_get_d` near the overflow and subnormal thresholds of `f64`, also at the exponent range of `f64` that `with_parse_range` there sets, and on results that overflow or underflow MPFR's exponent range, and check the exact results. Where the hypotheses state a special value only as a zero, of either sign, the tests also check the sign of that zero, which is more than the hypotheses assume. That tests the linked MPFR at these points only. The test `round_rational_oracle` there checks `round_rational` in `src/mpfr_calls/mpfr.rs`, which, like `with_parse_range`, only the tests compile, against what the MPFR manual says of `mpfr_set_q`, `mpfr_subnormalize` and `mpfr_get_d`, with exact and inexact rationals of both signs near the subnormal and overflow thresholds: `src/parse/diff_tests.rs` tests `rational_to_f64` against `round_rational`, and no theorem assumes anything of `round_rational`. Every hypothesis is an argument of the theorems, not an axiom:

- `range_ok emin emax`: MPFR's exponent range `[emin, emax]` contains `[-1073, 1024]`, so that every finite `f64` is an MPFR number of 53 bits. Outside the tests, neither `src/elementary.rs` nor `src/mpfr_calls/mpfr.rs` sets the range, which in an MPFR built thread-safe is that of the calling thread. MPFR's default range, `[-(2^30 - 1), 2^30 - 1]` (`MPFR_EMIN_DEFAULT` and `MPFR_EMAX_DEFAULT` in `mpfr.h`), contains it, but a program that narrows the range with `mpfr_set_emin` or `mpfr_set_emax`, for example through `gmp-mpfr-sys`, is not covered.
- `mpfr_format emin emax`: the reals that an MPFR number of 53 bits holds, 0 and `m × 2^e` with a significand `m` of 53 bits, `1/2 <= |m| < 1` and `emin <= e <= emax`, as MPFR has no subnormals (§4.2 "Nomenclature and Types" and `mpfr_get_emin` in §5.13). `f64_format` is the reals of finite `f64`s.
- `mpfr_round F rnd r v`: toward -∞, `v` is the largest element of `F` at or below `r`, or -∞ if there is none, and toward +∞ the least at or above `r`, or +∞. This is §4.4 "Rounding", which rounds toward -∞ to the number closest to `r` at or below it, and toward +∞ at or above it, read with the infinities among the numbers and the exponent range of §4.2, so that it covers overflow and underflow. §4.6 "Exceptions" says when these occur, but gives the result only for an overflow rounded to nearest, so the results toward -∞ and +∞, such as the largest MPFR number for a positive overflow rounded down, are read into §4.4. A result 0 may be a zero of either sign, as the theorems do not use it, and a result other than 0 is an `MNum`.
- `get_d_ok`: `mpfr_get_d` converts an MPFR number of 53 bits other than 0 to the `f64` that rounds it in the direction `rnd`, including overflow and the subnormal range of `f64`, a zero to a zero, and an infinity to the infinity of the same sign (`mpfr_get_d` in §5.4 "Conversion Functions"). The entry says only that it converts "using the rounding mode rnd", so that this rounding is to binary64 with its overflow and its subnormals, as IEEE 754 rounds, is read into it.
- `mpfr_correct emin emax D f g`: for a finite `f64` `x` whose real is in the domain `D`, `g rnd (with_val x)` is `f(x)` rounded in the direction `rnd` to an MPFR number of 53 bits. MPFR rounds every function correctly on its domain (§4.4, and §4.5 "Floating-Point Values on Special Numbers": "When the input point is in the domain of the mathematical function, the result is rounded as described in Rounding"). The entries of the functions in §5.7 "Transcendental Functions" say which mathematical function each computes, and `Elementary.v` defines it.
- `<f>_ok`, such as `exp_ok`, is `mpfr_correct` for the function, its point function and its domain, and the values at the special arguments that `src/elementary.rs` passes it, which follow from the rules of §4.5 for a point outside the domain but in its closure, the limit, and from the entries of §5.7: `exp`, `exp2` and `exp10` give +0 at -∞ and +∞ at +∞, `ln`, `log2` and `log10` give -∞ at ±0 and +∞ at +∞, `sinh` and `asinh` give -∞ and +∞ at -∞ and +∞, `tanh` gives -1 and 1 at -∞ and +∞, `atan` gives `-π/2` rounded down and `π/2` rounded up, `cosh` gives +∞ at either infinity, `acosh` gives +∞ at +∞, and `atanh` gives -∞ at -1 and +∞ at 1. For the one zero among these values, +0 at -∞, `exp_ok`, `exp2_ok` and `exp10_ok` assume only a zero, of either sign: the code passes the result of each of these MPFR functions only to `mpfr_get_d`, and `get_d_ok` says only that it converts a zero to a zero, so the theorems cannot use the sign. The same holds for the zeros of `pow_ok`, `pown_ok` and `atan2_ok` below. Only the directions in which the code passes these arguments are assumed: `f_rd` gets -∞, -1 or a zero, and `f_ru` +∞, 1, or, for `cosh`, -∞. `acos_ok` and `asin_ok` are `mpfr_correct` alone, as the code passes these functions only reals of `[-1, 1]`, and so are `cos_ok`, `sin_ok` and `tan_ok`, as the code passes `cos`, `sin` and `tan` only finite floats. The domain in `tan_ok` is `tan_dom`.
- `mpfr_correct2 emin emax D f g` is `mpfr_correct` for an MPFR function `g` of two numbers, a function `f` of two reals and a domain `D` of points of R². `pow_ok` is `mpfr_correct2` for `mpfr_pow`, `Rpow` and `pow_dom`, which also covers `pow(-0, t)` for `t > 0`, as the real of -0 is 0, and a zero result may have either sign. It adds the values that the `mpfr_pow` entry of §5.7 gives at the points other than these that `Interval::pow` passes: `pow(+0, t)` is +∞ for `t` below 0 or -∞ and +0 for `t` = +∞, `pow(r, ±0)` is 1 for `r` = +0 or +∞, `pow(+1, ±∞)` is 1, `pow(r, -∞)` is +∞ for `0 < r < 1` and +0 for `r > 1`, `pow(r, +∞)` is +0 for `0 < r < 1` and +∞ for `r > 1`, and `pow(+∞, t)` is +0 for `t` below 0 or -∞ and +∞ for `t` above 0 or +∞. Where the entry gives +0, `pow_ok` assumes only a zero, of either sign. `pown_ok` states the same of `mpfr_pow_si`, which that entry covers too: for an `n` with `is_i32 n` and a finite `x` whose real is in `pown_dom n`, `mpfr_pow_si rnd (with_val x) n` is `powerRZ x n` rounded in the direction `rnd`, and at the other points that `Interval::powi` passes, `pow_si(±0, n)` for `n < 0` is +∞ for an even `n` and the infinity with the sign of the zero for an odd one, `pow_si(+∞, n)` is +0 for `n < 0`, 1 for `n = 0` and +∞ for `n > 0`, and `pow_si(-∞, n)` for an odd `n` is -0 for `n < 0` and -∞ for `n > 0`, except that where the entry gives +0 or -0, `pown_ok` assumes only a zero. Unlike the special values of `<f>_ok` above, which are assumed only in the directions in which the code passes their arguments, these are assumed for both directions, as MPFR returns each of them exactly, whatever the direction. `is_i32 n` in `Model.v` says that `n` is in the range of `i32`, which `c_long` contains on every platform. `atan2_ok` is `mpfr_correct2` for `mpfr_atan2`, `Ratan2` and the points `(y, x)` with `y` other than 0 or `x` above 0, which covers `atan2(±0, x)` for `x > 0`, and adds the values that the `mpfr_atan2` entry of §5.7 gives at the points with one infinity that `Interval::atan2` passes, except that at `(y, +∞)` it assumes only a zero, of either sign, as `exp_ok` does at -∞: `atan2(y, +∞)` is a zero for a finite `y`, `atan2(y, -∞)` is π for a finite `y > 0` and -π for a finite `y < 0`, and `atan2(+∞, x)` and `atan2(-∞, x)` are π/2 and -π/2 for a finite `x`. These are assumed for both directions: the zeros are exact, and π, -π, π/2 and -π/2 are rounded in the direction `rnd`. The entry gives `atan2(y, +∞)` as +0 for a finite `y > 0` and -0 for a finite `y < 0`. For `y` = ±0, it gives ±0 by its `atan2(±0, x) = ±0` for `x > 0` if that `x` can be +∞. If it cannot, no bullet of the entry names the point, and the rule of §4.5 for a point outside the domain but in its closure gives the limit, 0. The wording favours the first reading: the bullets with one infinite argument say "for finite x", "for finite y > 0" or "for finite y < 0", and those of `atan2(±0, x) = ±0` say only "for x > 0". The entry cites the ISO C99 and IEEE 754 standards for its special values. C99 gives those of `atan2` in its Annex F, whose F.9.1.4, in the draft N1256 of C99 (ISO/IEC 9899:TC3), says `atan2(±0, x) returns ±0 for x > 0` and `atan2(±y, +∞) returns ±0 for finite y > 0`, with the same two readings and the same contrast. MPFR's `src/atan2.c` lists the bullets of F.9.1.4, with the same values and conditions, in a comment (l.66-79 in MPFR 4.2.2) that names no source, in another order, after the footnote of their first bullet. The unit test `atan2_ok` in `src/elementary/mpfr_tests.rs` also checks, at a few `y`, that the linked MPFR returns the zero with the sign of `y`, as `src/atan2.c` does for every finite `y`. The domain of `mpfr_correct2` leaves out the points with `y = 0` and `x <= 0`, so also the half-line `y = 0`, `x < 0` of `atan2_dom`, as the entry gives `atan2(±0, x) = ±π` for `x < 0`, by the sign of the zero, which no function of the reals gives. The code never passes them (see `atan2_correct` above). The values that `pow_ok` and `atan2_ok` add at a finite argument other than 0, such as `pow(r, -∞)` for `0 < r < 1` or `atan2(y, -∞)` for `y > 0`, are stated, as `mpfr_correct2` is, only where that argument is the real of a finite `f64` (`with_val`), as the code passes it, not at every real.

[`Contract.v`](theories/Contract.v) states what each field of a record `point` must do at the level of `f64`s, and has to be checked by reading, as `Mpfr.v` is. `point_correct` and `point_correct2` are the counterparts of `mpfr_correct` and `mpfr_correct2`: for finite `f64`s whose reals are in the domain, the field rounds the function of those reals in the direction `rnd` to an `f64`. Each `<f>_c`, such as `exp_c`, transcribes `<f>_ok` from MPFR numbers to `f64`s: an argument `with_val x` becomes `x`, a zero or an infinity of MPFR the `f64` zero or infinity of the same sign, the MPFR number 1 or -1 the `f64` 1 or -1, a result rounded to an MPFR number of 53 bits one rounded to an `f64`, and a special value that `<f>_ok` states only as a zero, of some sign, stays only a zero. [`MpfrPoint.v`](theories/MpfrPoint.v) shows that `mpfr_point m` does what `Contract.v` states where `m` satisfies `Mpfr.v`'s hypotheses: `mpfr_<f>_c : forall emin emax m, range_ok emin emax -> get_d_ok emin emax m -> <f>_ok emin emax m -> <f>_c (p_<f> (mpfr_point m))`. Of the theorems about the elementary functions, only `exp_correct_point`, `exp2_correct_point`, `exp10_correct_point`, `sinh_correct_point`, `tanh_correct_point`, `cosh_correct_point`, `ln_correct_point`, `log2_correct_point`, `log10_correct_point`, `asinh_correct_point`, `acosh_correct_point`, `atanh_correct_point`, `atan_correct_point`, `asin_correct_point`, `acos_correct_point`, `atan2_correct_point`, `sin_correct_point`, `cos_correct_point` and `tan_correct_point` are stated for a record `p : point`, with the hypothesis `<f>_c (p_<f> p)`, such as `exp_c (p_exp p)`, and also `sin_c (p_sin p)` and `cos_c (p_cos p)` for the last three, and they have no hypothesis about MPFR. `kernel_exp_correct` and the other `kernel_<f>_correct` of `DyPoint.v` are stated for `kernel_point p`, with no hypothesis about `p` or MPFR. The others are stated for MPFR functions `m`, through `mpfr_point m`, and what they assume of `m` is stated in `Mpfr.v`.

[`Elementary.v`](theories/Elementary.v) defines the point functions that are not in Stdlib, by the usual formulas: `Rexp2` and `Rexp10` as `Rpower 2` and `Rpower 10`, `Rlog2` and `Rlog10` as `Rlog 2` and `Rlog 10`, i.e. `ln r / ln 2` and `ln r / ln 10`, `Racosh r` as `ln (r + sqrt (r * r - 1))` and `Ratanh r` as `ln ((1 + r) / (1 - r)) / 2`, and the domains `real_dom`, all of R, `log_dom`, `(0, +∞)`, `asin_dom` and `acos_dom`, `[-1, 1]`, `acosh_dom`, `[1, +∞)`, `atanh_dom`, `(-1, 1)`, and `tan_dom`, R without the odd multiples of π/2, those of Table 9.1 of the draft P1788/D9.5. It defines `Rpow r t`, IEEE 1788's `pow`, as `Rpower r t`, i.e. `exp(t ln r)`, for `r > 0` and 0 otherwise, with the domain `pow_dom` of that table, the points `(r, t)` with `r > 0`, or with `r = 0` and `t > 0`, and the domain `pown_dom n` of `pown(x, n)`, all of R for `n >= 0` and R without 0 for `n < 0`. It defines `Ratan2 r t`, IEEE 1788's `atan2(y, x)` with `r` as `y` and `t` as `x`, by the usual formula: `atan(r / t)` for `t > 0`, that plus π for `t < 0` and `r >= 0`, and minus π for `t < 0` and `r < 0`, and π/2 or -π/2 by the sign of `r` for `t = 0`, with the domain `atan2_dom` of that table, R² without `(0, 0)`. The table defines `atan2(y, x)` as the principal value of the argument of `(x, y)`, and `Ratan2_range`, `Ratan2_polar` and `Ratan2_polar_unique` in `AtanReal.v` show that `Ratan2` is that angle, so the formula can be checked against the table's definition by those lemmas as well as by reading. Stdlib's `exp`, `ln`, `sin`, `cos`, `tan`, `sinh`, `cosh`, `tanh`, `arcsinh`, `atan`, `asin` and `acos` are IEEE 1788's `exp`, `log`, `sin`, `cos`, `tan`, `sinh`, `cosh`, `tanh`, `asinh`, `atan`, `asin` and `acos`, and Stdlib's `powerRZ r n`, as a function of `r`, is IEEE 1788's `pown(x, n)`. Like `Ieee1788.v`, it has to be checked by reading. A function defined only on a domain is a function from R to R, or from R² to R for `pow` and `atan2`, whose values outside the domain the theorems do not use.

[`Ieee1788.v`](theories/Ieee1788.v) states what IEEE Std 1788-2015 asks of an interval version of a function: the hull of the image (`is_hull`), or the tightest interval that contains it (`is_tightest`) where its bounds need not be floats, or an interval that contains it (`encloses`), the accuracy that IEEE 1788 calls valid, which `cos_encloses`, `sin_encloses` and `tan_encloses` state on their own, and the properties of the decorations. It and `Elementary.v` are the only files that have to be checked against the standard by reading, its `two_output` against the doc comment of `Interval::mul_rev_to_pair`, and its `cancel_result` against that of `Interval::cancel_minus`. For `com`, the standard asks for the computed interval to be bounded. `p`, for functions of one real, asks for the image instead. `is_hull_bounded` in `Integer.v` shows that the two are the same for a hull, which each function stated with `p` returns. `p2`, for functions of two reals, asks for the computed interval, as the standard does, as `x + y` can overflow where the image is bounded. Stdlib's `continuity_pt` and `continue_in` are for functions of one real, so for functions of two, `Ieee1788.v` defines continuity on R² itself, by ε and δ with the norm `max(|r|, |t|)` (`continue_in2`, `continuity2_pt`). It also defines the image of a box (`image2`) and the decorations of a function of two reals (`p2`, `strongest2`). For a function of one real that is defined only on a set of reals, its domain, such as `sqrt` on `[0, +∞)` (`sqrt_dom`), it defines the image of the reals of an interval that are in the domain (`image_dom`) and the decorations (`p_dom`, `strongest_dom`). `def`, `dac` and `com` ask for the interval to be a nonempty subset of the domain, and `com` for continuity at each of its points relative to the domain, as IEEE 1788 counts `sqrt` as continuous at 0. For `com`, `p_dom`, like `p2`, asks for the computed interval to be bounded. `sqr` is stated with these too, with the domain `sqr_dom`, which is all of R, because a square can overflow where the image is bounded, and so is `recip`, with the domain `recip_dom`, the reals other than 0. For a function of two reals whose domain is a product `D1 × D2` of two sets of reals, such as `/` on `div_dom1 × div_dom2`, i.e. R × (R ∖ {0}), it defines the image of the points of a box that are in the domain (`image2_dom`) and the decorations (`p2_dom`, `strongest2_dom`), as `image_dom`, `p_dom` and `strongest_dom` do for one real. A box is a nonempty subset of such a domain exactly when each of its sides is a nonempty subset of the matching factor, and `continue_in2` already takes a product of two sets, so `p2_dom` can state continuity relative to the domain as it states continuity relative to a box. A product suffices for `/`. For a function of two reals whose domain is any set `D` of points of R², such as `pow` and `atan2`, whose domains `pow_dom` and `atan2_dom` are not products, it defines the image of the points of a box that are in `D` (`image2_set`), continuity at a point relative to `D` (`continue_in2_set`), and the decorations (`p2_set`, `strongest2_set`), where `dac` asks for the restriction of the function to the box to be continuous, and `com` for continuity at each point of the box relative to `D`, as `p2_dom` does for a product. [`Dom2.v`](theories/Dom2.v) shows that for a product `D1 × D2` these are `image2_dom`, `continue_in2`, `p2_dom` and `strongest2_dom`, and [`DecBinary.v`](theories/DecBinary.v) that for all of R² `image2_set`, `p2_set` and `strongest2_set` are `image2`, `p2` and `strongest2`. For a function of three reals that is defined on all of R³, such as `fma`, which is `Rfma`, `r * t + u`, it defines continuity on R³ as on R², with the norm `max(|r|, |t|, |u|)` (`continue_in3`, `continuity3_pt`), the image of a box `x × y × z` (`image3`), and the decorations (`p3`, `strongest3`), where `com`, as in `p2`, asks for the computed interval to be bounded. For the constant function `c()` of a real `c`, a function of no arguments, it defines the decorations (`p0`, `strongest0`). R⁰ has one point, the empty tuple, so its only box is R⁰ itself, which is nonempty, bounded and the domain of `c()`, and `c()` is continuous on it. So `trv`, `def` and `dac` always hold, `com` asks for the computed interval to be bounded, as Annex B1 of the draft P1788/D9.5 says for a constant, and `ill` never holds, as it asks for an empty domain, which only NaN, the function of no arguments that is defined nowhere, has. For two-output division, `mulRevToPair(b, c)`, it defines the set `mul_rev b c` of reals `z` with `t * z` in `c` for some `t` in `b`, the convex sets of reals (`convex`), the connected components of a set of reals, which are its maximal nonempty convex subsets (`component`), and the outputs that the doc comment of `Interval::mul_rev_to_pair` describes for a set (`two_output`): both `EMPTY` for an empty set, the tightest interval that contains its only component and `EMPTY` for a set with one component, and the tightest intervals that contain its two components, the lower one first, for a set with two. For cancellative subtraction, `cancelMinus(x, y)`, it defines, for sets of reals `X` and `Y`, `Y + Z ⊇ X` (`sum_superset`), when cancelMinus has a value (`cancel_defined`): for an empty `X` and a bounded `Y`, and for `X = [a, b]` and `Y = [c, d]` with `d - c <= b - a`, and the result that the doc comment of `Interval::cancel_minus` describes (`cancel_result`): if cancelMinus has a value, the `valid` interval `z` with `Y + z ⊇ X` that every other `valid` interval with this property contains, and `ENTIRE` otherwise. `X` and `Y` are sets rather than intervals so that `cancelPlus(x, y)`, which is `cancelMinus(x, -y)`, is stated with the negations of the reals of `y` rather than with the model of `-y`. For interval literals, it defines the set of reals that an `NInterval` denotes (`ninterval_set`), with `above_num` and `below_num` for the reals at or above a lower bound and at or below an upper bound, where -∞ and +∞ bound nothing on their side and a lower bound +∞ or an upper bound -∞ leaves no real, as in `NInterval::EMPTY`. The theorems state textToInterval's result as the tightest interval that contains that set, the hull that §12.12.7 of the draft P1788/D9.5 asks for in an IEEE 754 binary64 type.

The theorems depend on four axioms. Three come from Stdlib's real numbers: `sig_forall_dec`, `sig_not_dec` and `functional_extensionality_dep`. The fourth is excluded middle (`classic`), which Flocq uses. `make` fails if [`Assumptions.v`](Assumptions.v) finds any other axiom, e.g. a proof left `Admitted`. It does not print the `Example`s of `MpfrExamples.v`, each of which applies a theorem that it prints, or a constructor of `made` or `dmade`, of which it prints `made_valid`, to `ideal emin emax` and to proofs from `range_ok` and `ideal_mpfr_ok`, which it prints too.

## What is not proved yet

- The SIMD backends that vectorize the same algorithm, such as `src/simd/x86_64/lanewise.rs` and `src/simd/wasm32.rs`. If the fast path rejects either lane, `mul_ru` of the `fma`, `sse42` and `wasm32` backends calls `mul_ru_tiny`, which rounds a product below `2^-968` as `mul1_ru_tiny` does, but differs from it in three ways. It computes three values in both lanes and selects one of them in each lane: the result for a zero `RN(x * y)`, the fast path's rounding of one product, which is `x * y` in a lane that the fast path accepts and the scaled product in one that it rejects, and that rounding divided by `2^128` and rounded upward. `mul1_ru_tiny` computes only those that its lane needs. It tells the sign of a zero `RN(x * y)` by comparing its bit pattern with that of +0 as signed integers. And it does not choose the lanes by the exponent of `RN(x * y)`, as `mul1_ru_tiny` does: it accepts a rejected lane if `RN(x * y)` is zero or `two_prod_is_exact` holds for the scaled product, which it checks at run time, and otherwise has the portable backend's `mul_ru` recompute both lanes.
- The other backends' versions of the functions that `abs`, `cancel_minus`, `cancel_plus`, `floor`, `ceil`, `max`, `min`, `mul_add`, `mul_rev_to_pair`, `recip`, `round`, `round_ties_even`, `sign`, `sqr`, `sqrt`, `trunc`, `-x`, `x + y`, `x - y`, `x * y`, `x / y`, `convex_hull` and `intersection` of intervals, the 21 functions of `src/elementary.rs` above, of which `cos`, `sin` and `tan` also call `x / y` and `floor`, `powi` `abs`, and `atan2` `classify` and `-x`, `TryFrom<(f64, f64)>`, `try_from_be_bytes` and its siblings, and `From<DecNInterval>`, `From<NInterval>` and `try_from_ninterval_exact`, which call `TryFrom<(f64, f64)>`, and the first two also `set_dec`, call: `floor`, `ceil`, `round`, `round_ties_even`, `trunc` and `neg0`, such as `_mm_floor_pd` on x86-64, `splat`, `gt`, `lt`, `and` and `or` in `sign`, `ge`, `le`, `bitmask`, `splat`, `max`, `swap` and `shuffle02` in `abs` and `classify`, `max`, `maximum`, `min`, `minimum` and `shuffle03` in `max` and `min`, `swap` in `-x` and `x - y`, `add_ru` in `x + y` and `x - y`, `max`, `mul_ru`, `neg0`, `shuffle02`, `shuffle13`, `splat` and `swap` in `x * y`, all but `shuffle13` also in `sqr`, `max`, `mul_add_ru`, `neg0`, `shuffle02`, `shuffle13` and `swap` in `mul_add`, `sqrt1_ru`, `sqrt_rd_ru` and `neg0` in `sqrt`, `div_ru`, `neg0`, `shuffle02`, `shuffle03`, `splat` and `swap` in `x / y`, `div_ru`, `shuffle02`, `shuffle13`, `splat` and `swap` in `recip`, `div_ru`, `neg0`, `shuffle02`, `shuffle03`, `shuffle13`, `splat` and `swap` in `mul_rev_to_pair`, `sub_ru`, `hadd_rn`, `extract`, `constant`, `max`, `min`, `shuffle02`, `shuffle13`, `sub_rn` and `add_rn` in `cancel_minus`, which `cancel_plus` calls on the result of `-x`, `le`, `all`, `neg0` and `splat` in `contains`, which the decorated `recip`, `x / y` and `mul_rev_to_pair` call, `lt`, `all`, `splat`, `extract0` and `extract1` in `is_empty`, `is_singleton`, `is_common_interval`, `inf_raw` and `sup_raw`, `max` in `convex_hull`, `min` in `intersection`, which `acos`, `acosh`, `asin`, `atanh`, the logarithms and `pow` call, `eq`, `all`, `splat`, `shuffle02` and `shuffle13` in `interior`, which `atanh`, the logarithms and `pow` call, `le` and `all` in `subset`, which `acos`, `acosh`, `asin` and `pow` call, and `constant` in `with_infsup_raw`, which `sqrt`, the functions of `src/elementary.rs`, `TryFrom<(f64, f64)>`, and `try_from_be_bytes` and its siblings call. The roundings are meant to be the same IEEE 754 operations in every backend, but the theorems are proved only for the portable backend's. `rational_to_f64` in `src/parse.rs`, which those three conversions call through `number_to_f64`, calls the portable backend's `round_up_exact` on every backend, as `src/simd.rs` imports that on each, so its theorem holds on every backend. So does `to_f64` of `src/dyadic.rs`.
- That Ziv's strategy in `src/dyadic.rs` returns, rather than panics at its cap of 4,096 bits: `ziv_correct` is about the number that it returns, and `ziv_at_passes` shows only that a level passes once its enclosure is narrow enough. So neither is it proved that `exp`, `exp2`, `exp10`, `sinh`, `cosh` and `tanh` of `src/dyadic/exp.rs`, `ln`, `log2`, `log10`, `asinh`, `acosh` and `atanh` of `src/dyadic/log.rs`, `atan`, `atan2`, `asin` and `acos` of `src/dyadic/atan.rs`, or `sin`, `cos` and `tan` of `src/dyadic/trig.rs`, return rather than panic, at the cap or elsewhere: their theorems cover the calls that return (see "What is assumed"). For the logarithms, it would take, at each argument that no rule settles, other than 1 for `ln` and `acosh` and the powers of 2 for `log2`, whose enclosures are exact, that the result is not an `f64`, so that `ziv_at_passes` applies, and that the enclosures are narrow enough by 4,096 bits. That log2 x and log10 x are not `f64`s there follows from the factorization of integers, as an `f64` is rational. For ln and atanh, it takes that e^r is irrational for every rational r other than 0, and for asinh and acosh, which are `ln` of an algebraic number other than 1, that e^r is not algebraic for those r, the theorem of Hermite and Lindemann. The inverse trigonometric functions need the same, at each argument that no rule settles, other than 1 for `acos`, whose enclosure is exact. For `atan` and `atan2`, it takes that tan r is irrational for every rational r other than 0, the theorem of Lambert, for `asin` and `acos`, that sin r and cos r are irrational for those r, and for `pi_times`, that π times a rational other than 0 is irrational, which follows from `pi_irrational` above. The trigonometric functions need, at each argument that no rule settles, that sin r, cos r and tan r are irrational for every rational r other than 0, which are the claims above for `asin`, `acos` and `atan`, and also that no `f64` lies so close to a multiple of π/2 other than 0 that `tan` panics in `Encl::div`, which takes a distance below 2^-127 from an odd multiple, or that the enclosures, whose width there comes from that of `reduce`, are still too wide at 4,096 bits. The least such distance is about 2^-60.9, at 6381956970095103 × 2^797, the case of Kahan and McDonald, and a search of every binade for the hard cases of `src/dyadic/trig/tests.rs` found none closer. They also need that `reduce` passes its `assert!`, `|ρ| <= 1`, and that the enclosure of sin ρ in `tan` lies inside (-1, 1), so that the square root that gives cos ρ does not panic and the enclosure of cos ρ, by which `tan` divides at an even `k`, is above 0. Both hold as `reduce` chooses `k` so that `|ρ|` is at most π/4 plus the width of the reduction, and sin(π/4) is below 0.71. Other than `pi_irrational`, none of this is proved here. Nothing but the tests of `src/dyadic.rs`, `src/dyadic/exp.rs`, `src/dyadic/log.rs`, `src/dyadic/atan.rs` and `src/dyadic/trig.rs` calls the kernel yet: `src/mpfr_calls.rs` still computes the elementary functions with MPFR, so the theorems of `DyPoint.v` are not yet about any function that the intervals call.
- The `nom` grammar of `src/parse.rs`, the first step of `FromStr` for intervals and decorated intervals, `interval!(s)`, `dec_interval!(s)` and `interval_exact!`, which reads an interval literal and computes its bounds as `Rat`s, exact rationals `(-1)^neg * num * 2^e2 * 5^e5 / den`. The ITF1788 tests in `tests/itf1788_tests` and the tests in `src/parse.rs` test it. That it returns only `NInterval`s with `ninterval_ok`, which the theorems about `From<NInterval>` and `From<DecNInterval>` assume, and so with `number_ok` of both bounds, which the theorem about `try_from_ninterval_exact` assumes, is checked by reading. The `DecNInterval`s that it returns then have `dec_ninterval_ok`, which the theorem about `From<DecNInterval>` assumes, by `dn_NAI_ok`, `dn_new_ok` and `dn_decorated_ok` for the step that decorates a literal, and by reading for the `nom` combinators that pass each literal to that step (see "What is assumed").
- `Nat::from_dec`, `Nat::from_hex` and `-` of `src/nat.rs`, with which the grammar computes the bounds of a literal, and `Nat::to_dec`, with which `src/format.rs` writes the decimal digits, are not modelled. That the first three return no zero limb at the top is checked by reading (see "What is assumed"), and the tests in `nat.rs` compare them and the other operations of `Nat` with `rug::Integer`. Nor is it proved that `impl Ord for Rat` in `src/parse/rat.rs`, which `number_le` takes as the order of the values of two `Rat`s, compares their values: the tests in `rat.rs` compare it with `rug::Rational` for small exponents, and for exponents up to `i32::MAX` in magnitude with bounds that MPFR computes, where those decide the order.
- The text that `Display`, `LowerExp` and `LowerHex` of intervals and decorated intervals write, in `fmt_impl`, `bound`, `fixed_text`, `sci_text` and `hex_text` of `src/format.rs`, apart from the digits that the functions of `src/format/digits.rs` compute: that `fmt_impl` passes `up = false` for the lower bound and `up = true` for the upper one, which is checked by reading, and the text that these functions write from the digits `d` and the exponent `k`, with the decimal digits that `Nat::to_dec` gives: the point, the zeros, the exponent, the digits of a rounding that carries into a new digit, the minus sign, which `bound` writes for every negative bound, -0 included, the text of 0 and of the infinities, which they write without `src/format/digits.rs`, that of the empty interval, the entire interval and NaI, the decoration and the width. The tests in `src/format/diff_tests.rs` compare the text of each bound, in both directions, with that of `fmt_bound_mpfr` in `src/mpfr_calls/mpfr.rs`, which only the tests compile, and which prints it with `mpfr::asprintf` as inari did before: for random bit patterns, integers, numbers beside a decimal carry or with hex digits at an edge, and other edge cases, each also negated, without a precision and with the precisions 0 to 20, and for some of them 40, 100, 330, 1100 and 65535. They compare `format!` of random intervals and decorated intervals, with and without a width and a precision, with the bounds of MPFR in the layout of `fmt_impl`, and parse the text back: it parses, contains the interval and keeps the decoration, or gives `dac` for `com` where the text of a bound is beyond `f64::MAX`, and `{:x}` without a precision gives the interval back exactly. In these tests, MPFR's text differs only for `{:.0x}` of a positive upper bound or a negative lower bound of at most 4 significant bits, where the comparison of bounds asks that the texts differ and that of whole intervals skips them (see "What is proved"). The test `format` in `src/format.rs` checks the text of some intervals.
- Every other operation, such as `mid` of intervals.

## Building

You need Rocq 9.1, its Stdlib and Flocq 4.2.2. With [opam](https://opam.ocaml.org):

```bash
opam repo add rocq-released https://coq.inria.fr/opam/released
opam install rocq-core.9.1.1 rocq-stdlib.9.0.0 coq-flocq.4.2.2
make -C rocq
```

You can also use Docker, as CI does ([`ci-rocq.yml`](../.github/workflows/ci-rocq.yml)). The container's user (uid 1000) must be able to write to `rocq/`.

```bash
docker run --rm -v "$PWD/rocq:/w" -w /w rocq/rocq-prover:9.1 \
  bash -c 'opam install -y coq-flocq.4.2.2 && make'
```

Warnings are errors. The `Makefile` runs its own recipes, though not those of the `RocqMakefile` that it generates, under bash with `-euo pipefail`; these flags take GNU Make 3.82 or later, and an older GNU Make runs the recipes under bash without them. `make clean` removes the build outputs.
